# Template gaps

What the hatchkit starter template does **not** yet have, measured against a
reference project that was built on it and then carried to production across a
web client, two native shells, a browser extension, a launcher extension, an MCP
server, a public API and a self-host distribution.

## How to read this

Each row is one discrete, generic gap. Rows are grouped by the twelve follow-up
features that will close them; one sibling session owns each group.

Every row carries an **invariant** — the rule that fails *quietly* when the gap
is filled the wrong way. Those are the point of this document. A missing feature
is obvious from its absence; a feature implemented against the grain of one of
these invariants looks finished, passes review, and then loses somebody's data
or orphans somebody's session months later.

Rows state **mechanism**, never the reference project's domain. Where something
could not be separated from that domain it is listed under
"Domain-specific — do not extract" and stays out of the template on purpose.

`refPath` points into the reference implementation. It is evidence, not a file
to copy: the reference solved the problem for one product, and the template has
to solve it for any.

## Status

| value | meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `[ ]` | open — nothing in the starter addresses it |
| `[~]` | partial — something exists but does not meet the invariant |
| `[x]` | closed — landed in the starter, with the invariant covered by a test |
| `[-]` | dropped — deliberately out of scope, with the reason |

Tick a row only when the work is merged into `main` **and** the invariant has a
test. An untested implementation is `[~]`: these invariants all describe silent
failures, so "it seemed to work" is not evidence.

## Landing status

Audited against `main` on 2026-09-25, by reading the four surfaces a feature has
to appear in — the interactive stepper, the create flags, `hatchkit update`, and
the read-only state surface (`status` / `explain` / MCP) — plus what the
scaffolder writes.

Three have landed: `release`, `auth-account-security` and `workspaces`, each a
real member of the `Feature` union with scaffolding behind it. The other nine
have not — there is no i18n, extension, Raycast, token-auth, client-core,
public-API, dev-infra or self-host identifier on any branch. The *partial*
markers on rows 7–11 reflect pre-existing starter machinery, not a follow-up
session's work.

Sessions are landing while this is written, so re-read the `Feature` union in
`cli/src/prompts.ts` rather than trusting this paragraph's count.

So most rows here stay open, and cross-feature reconciliation has barely begun.
The mechanism is ready for it: `cli/src/features/contract.ts` for registration,
prerequisites and the ledger, and `cli/src/scaffold/identifiers.ts` for names.

The first reconciliation defect has already appeared and is fixed:
`auth-account-security` was in the `Feature` union and in `hatchkit update`'s
picker but not in `KNOWN_FEATURES`, so `hatchkit create --features
auth-account-security` rejected it as unknown while `update` offered it. **When
you add a feature, add it in all three places** — the union, `KNOWN_FEATURES`
(which is what `--features` and the `create --help` text read), and the stepper
— and confirm the read-only surface reports it. A feature reachable on one
surface and not another is the failure mode to watch for here.

### What a feature author already inherits

These are enforced for the six existing features and apply to every new one, so
a feature does not have to re-solve them:

- **A dry run that reaches the command line.** `hatchkit update --dry-run`
  reports the plan and writes nothing; `--features`, `--yes` and `--json` make
  the command scriptable. New writes should go through `FeatureLedger`, which
  makes the dry run a property of the writer rather than a flag each call site
  remembers.
- **Off-state coherence, checked mechanically.**
  `cli/test-feature-matrix.ts` scaffolds a combination matrix — bare, each
  feature alone, the full set, and the pairs that touch the same files — and
  asserts no dangling imports, no script pointing at a file that was not
  scaffolded, no release workflow for an absent shell, and no env var or compose
  service for an absent service. Add a row to its `expectations` table when a
  feature introduces an env var or a service.
- **A retrofit contract.** `cli/test-update-flags.ts` pins it: a dry run writes
  nothing, a second run is a no-op, and a hand-edited file is never overwritten.
- **Project state is reported.** `hatchkit status --json` carries
  `project.features`, `project.addableFeatures` and `project.signing`, so an
  agent reads the feature set instead of guessing at it.

## Prerequisites

The dependency order below is what the survey found, normalised onto the twelve
feature keys. **IDS** means the scaffold-time identifier resolution in
`cli/src/scaffold/identifiers.ts`, which has already landed — every area that
bakes a name into storage depends on it, and no area may introduce a name of its
own.

Declare these as `requires` on the feature definition rather than in prose; see
`docs/feature-authoring.md`. A prerequisite that is only written down is a
prerequisite that goes stale.

| --- | --- | --- | --- |

| 6 | [Raycast + MCP](#raycast-mcp) | absent | `client-core-offline`, `token-client-auth`, `server-platform-public-api`, **IDS** |

| 8 | [Mobile (Capacitor)](#mobile) | partial | **IDS**, `sign-in-methods`, `cross-platform-dev-infra`, `client-core-offline`, `desktop` |

| 12 | [i18n](#i18n) | absent | `client-core-offline`, `cross-platform-dev-infra`, `deploy-selfhost-ops`, `browser-extension` |

<a id="token-client-auth"></a>

## Token-client auth

**Key:** `token-client-auth` · **Starter today:** absent · **Open rows:** 31

The reference implements one single auth path for every client: a browser keeps a cookie, and every non-browser client (two native shells, a browser extension, a launcher extension, a CLI) keeps the SAME session token and sends it as `Authorization: Bearer <token>` (and as a `bearer.<token>` WebSocket subprotocol), so the server resolves cookie and token clients to identical session objects. Around that sit an RFC 8628 device-authorization flow with an allowlist of client ids, a self-reported client header used for labelling only, per-client-kind session lifetimes enforced through database hooks on both create and refresh, a devices list with per-session revocation, a once-a-minute re-check of every live socket that closes revoked ones with a shared numeric close code, and a per-host secret-store abstraction (OS keychain, encrypted file, session-scoped extension storage, encrypted launcher store) that refuses to persist where the platform's "encryption" is not encryption. The starter template has none of this: `starter/packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:23-75` registers better-auth with no plugins at all (no bearer, no device authorization, no second factor, no organization), `starter/packages/server/src/ws/auth.ts:9-19` authenticates an upgrade from cookies only and never re-checks, there is no devices router (`starter/packages/server/src/trpc/routers/` has billing/health/items/ml/profile), no client-kind or version fields on the session, no CORS `exposedHeaders` for the token header (`starter/packages/server/src/app.ts:24`), and `starter/packages/client/src/lib/auth-client.ts:50-52` is a bare `createAuthClient` with no token capture, no secret store and no `credentials: "omit"` path. Everything in this area is a from-zero build in the template.

### Gaps

- [ ] **Add a bearer-token session plugin so there is exactly one auth path**
  - Register the auth library's bearer plugin so an `Authorization: Bearer <session-token>` header resolves to the same session object a cookie would. There is no second credential type to mint, paste or expire separately: a non-browser client signs in the ordinary way and keeps the session token it is handed. Signature enforcement on the token must stay OFF, because two token shapes legitimately reach the server — a signed token from form sign-in and a raw token from the device grant — and enforcing signatures accepts only the first.
  - *Invariant:* By the time any resolver reads the session, a cookie client and a token client must be indistinguishable. The moment any code branches on how the credential arrived, there are two authorization surfaces and only one of them gets reviewed.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:264-279`
- [ ] **Expose the token response header through CORS**
  - The bearer plugin returns the freshly issued session token on a response header. A cross-origin client cannot read a response header unless the CORS layer lists it in `exposedHeaders`, and this applies to the uncredentialed CORS answer given to extension-scheme origins as well as to the ordinary one.
  - *Invariant:* If the header is not exposed, every cross-origin sign-in succeeds, returns 200, and hands the client nothing to store. The failure looks like a server that forgot to enable the plugin, not like a CORS mistake.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/app.ts:56,74`
- [ ] **Register the second-factor plugin BEFORE the token plugin**
  - After-hooks run in plugin registration order. A second-factor plugin creates a challenge by deleting the session that form sign-in just created. If the token plugin's hook runs first, it emits the token header for that about-to-be-deleted session. Pin the order with a test that reads the auth module's source, not just the behaviour.
  - *Invariant:* Wrong order: a token client stores a credential that answers 401 on every subsequent request, while sign-in itself reported success. The only symptom is "signed in, then immediately signed out", with no server error anywhere.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:257-279`
- [ ] **Only ever store a truthy token from the response header**
  - The token header is emitted only on responses that actually carry a session cookie — which is a small minority of authenticated calls. Every write of the stored credential must be guarded on the value being non-empty, at both the capture site in the auth client's response hook and the setter in the secret-store module.
  - *Invariant:* An unconditional write of whatever `headers.get()` returned erases a good token on the first session-check call. The app keeps working for the rest of the run from memory and comes back signed out on the next launch — a bug that never reproduces in the session that caused it.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/lib/native-session.ts:268-288; packages/client/src/lib/auth-client.ts:143-151`
- [ ] **Add the device-authorization (RFC 8628) flow with an allowlist and a front-end verification URI**
  - Register the device-authorization plugin with an explicit code lifetime, poll interval, a `validateClient` predicate backed by an allowlist of client ids, and a `verificationUri` built from the FRONT-END origin rather than the API's own. This is the only way in for a client that cannot show a credential form and for accounts where a second factor or a federated redirect cannot complete inside a shell.
  - *Invariant:* Left at its default the verification URI points at the API origin, which serves no such page: the user is sent to a 404 and the waiting client polls forever. Any deployment that splits API and front end (which is every deployment, including local development) breaks silently.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:313-329`
- [ ] **Put the client auth helpers in the framework-free shared package, with a single-shot exchange beside the poll loop**
  - One module exporting form sign-in, sign-up, device-authorization start, a SINGLE exchange attempt, a poll loop over that attempt, and a best-effort remote sign-out — over an injectable fetch, with no framework or DOM imports. The single-shot exchange is not an optimisation: a host whose process is suspended between two polls (an event-page worker stopped after ~30 idle seconds) cannot hold a loop open and must drive one attempt from whatever wakes it.
  - *Invariant:* If only the loop is exported, the suspendable host writes its own exchange and the two copies drift on the retry codes. A non-terminal code treated as terminal aborts a flow the user is about to approve; a terminal code treated as non-terminal polls a denied code until it expires.
  - *Reference:* `packages/core/src/session-auth.ts:1-372`
- [ ] **Treat a missing token on a successful sign-in as an error**
  - When sign-in returns 200 but no token header, the helper must throw a named error rather than returning a session without a credential. Separately, a second-factor challenge response (a body flagging a redirect, with no session) must throw its own named code rather than reporting a missing token.
  - *Invariant:* Reporting "no token" for a second-factor account sends every future debugger after the plugin configuration instead of after the second factor. Returning success with an empty credential produces a client that believes it is signed in and 401s on everything.
  - *Reference:* `packages/core/src/session-auth.ts:166-182`
- [ ] **Self-reported client identification header, used for labelling only**
  - A header carrying a client id, narrowed server-side to a known client kind (anything unrecognised becomes `unknown`), plus a human label derived from the kind and, for browser-hosted clients, from the user agent. The device grant carries the id in its body instead of a header, so the resolver must fall back from header to body.
  - *Invariant:* The value is self-reported — a caller can claim to be any client. Branch on it for authorization even once and the whole allowlist becomes decorative. It may label a row and choose a session window, nothing else.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/auth/client-label.ts:1-47; packages/server/src/auth/session-hooks.ts:73-85`
- [ ] **Stamp client kind and version onto the session row as non-writable additional fields**
  - Add session fields for client kind, client release and protocol level, all declared as not accepted from the request body, filled by the create hook from the request, and moved forward on use through the auth library's own adapter so its hooks and any secondary storage see the change.
  - *Invariant:* A field the request body can write is a field a caller can forge to whatever the devices list will display, and (because the window is chosen from it) to whatever session lifetime it prefers. Writing it outside the library's adapter bypasses hooks and secondary storage, so one of the two copies goes stale.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:231-263,413-424`
- [ ] **Per-client-kind session lifetime, enforced on BOTH create and refresh**
  - Two windows — a long one for clients that store a token, a short one for browser cookie sessions — written by a `session.create.before` hook and re-written by a `session.update.before` hook. The library's global lifetime option is a single number, so the global must be set to the LONG value and the short one applied by hook; the refresh-due trigger is computed against the global. The refresh hook must return "no change" unless the update is already moving the expiry.
  - *Invariant:* Three quiet failures. Create hook alone: a shortened row is restored to the global value the first time it is used, so the split lasts exactly one refresh. Global set to the short value: a long row is not considered due for refresh until it is nearly expired, collapsing the long window to a few days. Rewriting expiry on every session update: unrelated writes (a scope switch) silently become session extensions.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/auth/session-lifetime.ts:1-127; packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:377-411`
- [ ] **Read the client for a refresh off the session ROW, never off the triggering request**
  - The refresh hook must take the client kind stamped on the session being refreshed, falling back to the request's header only when the library did not put the session on the context.
  - *Invariant:* Deciding from the live request lets a socket liveness probe — whose handshake carries no custom header, and which a browser cannot attach one to — quietly demote a long-lived client's session to the short window, and lets a browser session promote itself to the long window by sending a header on a refresh. Both are invisible until a device comes back from a week idle and is signed out.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/auth/session-hooks.ts:87-115`
- [ ] **Devices screen with per-session revocation, resolving the token server-side**
  - A router that projects the auth library's own session list into a wire shape, flags the caller's own row, and revokes by a client-facing id that is resolved to the session token on the server. Plus a "sign out everywhere else" mutation. Revocation must be scoped to the caller's own list.
  - *Invariant:* The session token IS the credential, and the library's list returns it while its revoke takes it. Ship the token to the client so it can be passed back to revoke, and the devices screen becomes a credential dump — every device's live token, in a response any XSS can read. Failing to scope the lookup turns another user's session id into a revocation oracle.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/trpc/routers/devices.ts:1-127`
- [ ] **Accept an upgrade token from three sources, query string last**
  - The socket upgrade must look for the credential in: an Authorization header (clients that can set headers), a `bearer.<token>` subprotocol (a browser's WebSocket constructor can set a subprotocol but not headers), and a query parameter as a last resort. The server must echo back any offered `bearer.*` subprotocol or the browser refuses the connection. The upgrade must keep the exact header set that authenticated it.
  - *Invariant:* Order matters because a query string is the one place a credential leaks into access logs, referrers and proxy traces — put it first and the safe paths are never exercised. Not keeping the authenticating headers means the periodic re-check has to rebuild the lookup, which is a second verification path that will drift from the first.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/ws/auth.ts:6-101`
- [ ] **Re-check the session behind every live socket on a timer**
  - A registry of open sockets, each with a probe that replays its own authenticating headers, swept on an interval; sockets whose session is gone are dropped out of their room and closed. The probe must disable the session cookie cache and disable refresh, and a probe that throws must count as `unknown`.
  - *Invariant:* Four separate quiet failures. No re-check: revoking a device stops its HTTP requests but its socket keeps streaming that user's events for days — a revocation hole, not a cosmetic one. Cache left on: a revoked cookie session keeps answering out of the cache, so the re-check reads a cache instead of the session table. Refresh left on: the probe extends the very session it is only supposed to be asking about, once per interval, forever. A throw read as "revoked": one bad database minute signs every connected device out at once.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/ws/session-watch.ts:1-97; packages/server/src/ws/auth.ts:103-139`
- [ ] **Put the revoked-session close code in the shared protocol package and latch on it**
  - One numeric close code defined once in the package both ends already import. The client must stop reconnecting permanently for that client instance rather than entering backoff, and call a host callback. Hosts recover by BUILDING A NEW client when their credential changes (keyed on the token), so nothing has to un-latch the old one.
  - *Invariant:* A number duplicated on two sides is a number that drifts, and a client that got it wrong reconnect-loops in silence behind a status dot that never settles. Entering backoff instead of latching is the same loop: the credential it was built with will never be accepted again.
  - *Reference:* `packages/shared/src/protocol.ts:193; packages/core/src/sync-client.ts:35-54,256-266`
- [ ] **Ask whether the credential is still live before signing the device out**
  - The revoked handler must first ask the session endpoint — with the cookie cache disabled — whether the credential held NOW has a live session, and resume with a fresh socket if it does. Only a clean "no session" forgets the credential, notifies and redirects. A check that throws proceeds to sign out.
  - *Invariant:* Some ordinary operations (toggling a second factor, changing a password) delete the current session and create a new one. The socket was opened with the old one, so the server correctly closes it — but the device is still signed in, and every tab of that browser shares the new credential. Without the re-ask, an ordinary settings change signs the user out of everything.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/lib/session-revoked.ts:55-69,94-101`
- [ ] **Forget the credential on revocation, but keep durable client-side queued work**
  - On revocation: count any durable queued client-side work FIRST (every later step can fail), then clear the credential in both places — the auth library's own store and the host secret store — then tell the user with the count, then land them on the sign-in screen. Never clear the queued work. Concurrent calls share one run and the handler is not latched forever.
  - *Invariant:* A revoked credential is a statement about this device's session, not a verdict on work the server has never seen; dropping it destroys the only copy. And keeping it silently looks identical from the outside to dropping it silently — which is why the count is stated on the sign-in screen rather than left to be discovered.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/lib/session-revoked.ts:1-148; packages/client/src/lib/revoke-this-device.ts:1-59`
- [ ] **One secret-store abstraction per host, with a hydration gate and a deadline**
  - A three-method store interface (get/set/delete) chosen per shell at first use — OS keychain on one, encrypted file behind an IPC bridge on another, memory-only session-scoped storage in a browser extension, the launcher's own encrypted store — loaded lazily so a web bundle never pulls a native package in. Hydration is published as an external store that consumers gate BEHAVIOUR on, not the component tree, and it must have a timeout that degrades to "signed out".
  - *Invariant:* Gating the component tree breaks a statically prerendered build: the prerender has no shell, so a component that renders nothing while hydrating disagrees with the served markup and the subtree is thrown away. And a store call that never answers, with no deadline, is a permanently frozen launch behind a splash that does not auto-hide — with no console on a device build.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/lib/native-session.ts:1-266`
- [ ] **Refuse to persist the credential where the platform's encryption is not encryption**
  - The desktop secret store must inspect the platform backend and refuse to write when it is a fallback that encrypts with a key compiled into the runtime. In that case the credential lives in memory for the run, the next launch starts signed out, any older ciphertext is removed, and the status is reported in the UI. Ciphertext that fails to decrypt (new keyring, profile copied to another machine) is deleted rather than retried forever. Writes are atomic and owner-only.
  - *Invariant:* The platform API reports success on the obfuscation-only backend, so a naive implementation looks secure and is not — a credential sitting in a file anyone who can read the user's home directory can decrypt. Leaving an older ciphertext behind a token that was not written means the next launch resumes a session the user believes was never stored.
  - *Reference:* `electron/src/secure-store.ts:1-143`
- [ ] **Detect a fresh install with a marker in a store that IS wiped on uninstall**
  - Keychain items survive deleting the app on some platforms; the preferences container does not. Write a marker in the container at first run, and on a launch that finds a stored credential with no marker, delete the credential before using it.
  - *Invariant:* Without this, delete-and-reinstall silently resumes the previous session — including a previous USER's session on a shared or handed-down device. It is a real authorization boundary failure that looks like a convenience feature.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/lib/native-session.ts:181-200`
- [ ] **Turn off cloud sync on the secret store item**
  - Where the platform keychain can replicate items to the account's other devices, explicitly disable synchronisation for the session item, tolerating platforms that refuse the call.
  - *Invariant:* A session credential replicated to the user's other devices means one row in the devices list covers several machines, so revoking that device revokes all of them and none of them individually — which is the entire point of a per-device list.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/lib/native-session.ts:144-152`
- [ ] **Token clients must suppress cookies on every request**
  - Once a stored token exists, the auth client and the API client both switch to `credentials: "omit"`. With no token — every browser request — the outgoing request must be byte-identical to what it was, and a test should assert that rather than assume it.
  - *Invariant:* A second, silent credential masks a broken token path completely: the app works, and nobody discovers the bearer path never worked until the cookie is dropped by a tracking-prevention rule, or a platform where no cookie is possible. The server-side tell is that a request from a shell must arrive with NO cookie header at all.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/lib/auth-client.ts:81-91; CLIENT-side rule stated at CLAUDE.md:670-673`
- [ ] **Bind a stored token to the origin that issued it**
  - Store the issuing origin beside the token (claiming the current origin for tokens stored before the field existed), read the token back only when the stored origin matches the configured one, and sign out against the ISSUING origin rather than the currently configured one.
  - *Invariant:* A session token is a credential for exactly one server. After the configured endpoint moves, sending it along hands this account's live session to a server with no business holding it — and signing out against the new server leaves the old session alive forever.
  - *Reference:* `packages/raycast/src/lib/auth.ts:20-75,97-115`
- [ ] **Persist a pending device authorization, serialize exchanges, and bind it**
  - For hosts that cannot hold a poll loop open, persist the pending authorization in a session-scoped store: request id, device code, issuing origin, who started it and for which user, expiry and interval. Every exchange runs through a serial chain, a half-written record decodes to "none", a record is only live while unexpired AND matching the current origin, and a wake-up alarm finishes what the foreground could not. The device code is a credential until spent, so it belongs in session-scoped storage, and a snapshot handed to any UI must carry the user-visible code and never the device code.
  - *Invariant:* Without the serial chain, two wake-ups (an approval message and a timer) spend one device code twice and one of them throws a terminal error that aborts a flow the user already approved. Without the origin binding, a code started against one server is exchanged against another. Persist the device code where it survives a browser restart and it outlives the browser session it was scoped to.
  - *Reference:* `packages/extension/src/lib/device-auth-store.ts:1-167; packages/extension/src/background/device-sign-in.ts:59-75`
- [ ] **Validate the approval URL's scheme before opening it, and confirm identity before adopting the token**
  - Open the verification URL only when it is https, or http on a loopback host. And because the device grant response names no user, resolve the issued token to its account with a session lookup before storing it; a token whose user is not the expected one is revoked and discarded.
  - *Invariant:* An unvalidated verification URL is an open redirect driven by a server response, opened in the user's browser. Adopting a token without confirming who it belongs to means a flow started for one account can silently end up holding another account's session — with no error at any step.
  - *Reference:* `packages/extension/src/background/device-sign-in.ts:94-138`
- [ ] **Approval page: claim the code before deciding, and handle the concurrent approver**
  - The approval endpoint refuses an approve or deny for a code that this session has not first claimed, with a generic invalid-request error. So the page must claim, then decide, and report a bad code exactly once. For callers where a second actor may approve the same code, treat "already approved" as success — but re-ask rather than trusting the message, because a DENIED code reports the same thing. The page must sit behind the protected route prefix.
  - *Invariant:* Approving without claiming fails with an error that reads to the user as a mistyped code, so they retype a perfectly good code until it expires. Trusting the "already processed" message turns a denial into a success.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/lib/device-approve.ts:1-112; packages/client/src/app/app/device/page.tsx:1-24`
- [ ] **Add the approval path to the post-sign-in redirect allowlist**
  - The sign-in redirect parameter must go through one validator that accepts only a single-slash same-origin path under an allowlisted set of prefixes — and that set must include the device-approval path, so a prefilled code survives the sign-in detour.
  - *Invariant:* The sign-in page is the one page everyone trusts, so an unvalidated redirect parameter is a phishing redirect. Omit the approval path from the allowlist and a user who follows a prefilled approval link while signed out is silently dropped somewhere else, with the code lost.
  - *Reference:* `CLAUDE.md:1305-1316`
- [ ] **Trust rule for browser-hosted token clients whose origin is random per install**
  - Some browser engines give every install a random per-install origin, which cannot be listed in advance. The rule must therefore be per-SCHEME, narrowed by two things: the request carries no session cookie, and the CORS answer for such an origin never allows credentials. One function answers for the auth library's trusted-origins option, the CORS delegate, the health endpoint's self-report and the socket upgrade. It is off unless the operator opts in via an environment switch.
  - *Invariant:* Four separate answers about one request WILL differ. And the cookie rule must key on the SESSION cookie by name, never on "any cookie at all": a socket upgrade cannot opt out of cookies, and a cross-site-eligible cookie set by a CDN rides along on every upgrade — so "any cookie" silently refuses the socket for every user behind such a CDN. Conversely, dropping the cookie condition lets any add-on in the browser open a socket on the signed-in person's cookie, since sockets are not subject to CORS.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/auth/extension-origins.ts:1-110`
- [ ] **Name the refusal when a token-only client cannot complete a second factor**
  - Where the second-factor challenge is carried by a signed cookie, a client that can neither read nor send cookies cannot complete it at all. Both the shared helper and the shell's own form must fail with a NAMED code and point the user at the device flow, whose approval happens in a browser that already passed the factor.
  - *Invariant:* Without a named code the client reports a generic sign-in failure for an account whose password was correct, and the user retypes it. The device flow is the escape hatch and must be offered at exactly that refusal.
  - *Reference:* `packages/core/src/session-auth.ts:45-46,166-174; CLAUDE.md:1087-1100`
- [ ] **Headless and CI launches must not touch the host secret store or the OS browser**
  - A headless run appends the platform's mock-secret-store flag, records URLs it would have opened instead of opening them, and — when no explicit data directory is given — uses a separate profile from the installed app's.
  - *Invariant:* Without the separate profile the mock key cannot decrypt the real profile's stored credential, and the store deletes what it cannot decrypt: a CI run silently signs the developer's installed app out. Without the URL capture, an automated approval test has no way to read the approval URL at all.
  - *Reference:* `CLAUDE.md:282-289`
- [ ] **Pin this area with integration tests against the real auth library**
  - Unit tests for the pure decisions (which client, which window, which verdict) plus integration tests that drive the REAL library: plugin registration order read from the source, session windows actually written to rows on create and on refresh, socket handshake spoof attempts over a real upgrade, revocation closing a live socket, and the device flow's retry codes against a fake auth server.
  - *Invariant:* "The hook returned the right value" and "the library wrote it" are different claims, and only the second one is the feature. Every failure in this area is silent — a token that 401s later, a window that resets on first use, a socket that keeps streaming — so nothing here is caught by a test that stops at the pure function.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/tests/two-factor-integration.test.ts; packages/server/src/tests/session-lifetime-integration.test.ts; packages/server/src/tests/ws-session-watch.test.ts; packages/core/src/tests/session-auth-device.test.ts`

### Identifiers this area freezes

Every name below becomes a contract the first time this area ships. All of
them must come from the manifest's `identifiers` block — see
`cli/src/scaffold/identifiers.ts`. An area that spells one of these out as a
literal has re-introduced the divergence that block exists to prevent.

- Client identification request header: `x-<product>-client` (packages/core/src/session-auth.ts:40, packages/server/src/auth/client-label.ts:9) — stamped on every session row ever created and read back by the devices screen
- Client release header: `x-<product>-client-version` (packages/shared/src/api-level.ts:107)
- Protocol level header: `x-<product>-api-level` (packages/shared/src/api-level.ts:110)
- Device-flow client ids, one per client surface: `<product>-raycast`, `<product>-cli`, `<product>-extension`, `<product>-desktop`, `<product>-mobile` (packages/server/src/auth/client-label.ts:12-18) — allowlisted server-side AND compiled into each shipped client; changing one breaks every installed copy of that client
- Host secret-store item key for the session credential: `<product>.session-token` (packages/client/src/lib/native-session.ts:50) — an OS keychain item that outlives uninstall
- Fresh-install marker key: `<product>.installed` (packages/client/src/lib/native-session.ts:56)
- Extension session-storage key: `<product>.session` (packages/extension/src/lib/session.ts:14)
- Extension pending-device-auth keys: `<product>.pending-device-auth`, `<product>.device-auth-failed-at`, `<product>.device-auth-error` (packages/extension/src/lib/device-auth-store.ts:24-26)
- Extension wake-up alarm name: `<product>.device-auth-poll` (packages/extension/src/lib/device-auth-store.ts:22)
- Launcher encrypted-store keys: `<product>.session.token`, `<product>.session.email`, `<product>.session.userId`, `<product>.session.server`, `<product>.originId` (packages/raycast/src/lib/auth.ts:17-27) — orphaning any of these orphans every install's stored credential
- Desktop encrypted credential filename: `session.bin` under the app's user-data directory (electron/src/secure-store.ts:29)
- Headless data-directory env var: `<PRODUCT>_USER_DATA_DIR` (CLAUDE.md:287)
- WebSocket revoked-session close code: 4401 (packages/shared/src/protocol.ts:193) — written by the server and read by every shipped client build
- WebSocket bearer subprotocol prefix: `bearer.` (packages/server/src/ws/auth.ts:7)
- Device-approval page path: `/app/device` (packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:325, packages/client/src/app/app/device/page.tsx) — baked into the verification URI printed by every waiting client and into the sign-in redirect allowlist
- Session `additionalFields` names: `client`, `clientVersion`, `clientApiLevel` (packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:236-262) — persisted column names on every existing session row
- Pinned Chromium extension id, derived from the signing key: `STORE_EXTENSION_ID` and the `chrome-extension://<id>` origin built from it (packages/shared/src/store-clients.ts:38,55)
- Origin-trust env switches: `TRUST_EXTENSION_ORIGINS`, implied by `TRUST_STORE_APPS` (packages/server/src/auth/extension-origins.ts:33-36) — in every self-hoster's env file
- Library-fixed, not ours, but must be referenced by one constant each: the `set-auth-token` response header and the `urn:ietf:params:oauth:grant-type:device_code` grant type (packages/core/src/session-auth.ts:43,300)

### Domain-specific — do not extract

- The running timer / `entries.current` mirror that the reference clears alongside the credential on account deletion and server switch (packages/client/src/lib/running-mirror.ts, CLAUDE.md:1192) — the generic form is 'a single authoritative piece of session-scoped state mirrored into durable storage', which belongs to the client-core area, not here
- The offline queue's contents being 'time the user tracked that no server has ever seen' — the generic invariant kept here is 'durable client-side queued work survives a revoked credential'; the queue mechanism itself is client-core-offline's area
- Per-entry / per-workspace scoping of account deletion: entries, catalog, favorites, invoices, import batches, invoice-attached rows (CLAUDE.md:1149-1160) — deletion cascade design belongs to multi-tenancy
- Literal `trackyourtime-*` client-id strings, the `x-trackyourtime-*` header names and the `X-TrackYourTime-*` webhook headers — carried into frozenIdentifiers as `<product>-*` shapes rather than transcribed
- The public REST surface's separate long-lived API tokens with frozen visibility ceilings and per-minute rate limits (packages/server/src/auth/api-token.ts, CLAUDE.md:1947-1992) — a DIFFERENT credential type from the session tokens surveyed here, and it belongs to server-platform-public-api
- Extension/launcher UI copy and menu-bar surfaces ('Today', 'Continue', 'Show All Time', the timer badge) that gate on the signed-in user (CLAUDE.md:255-260)
- The workspace/organization plugin and its `WorkspaceMember` mirror, which appear in the same auth module but are multi-tenancy's
- Second-factor plugin internals, email verification gating on a configured transport, the federated sign-in button's per-shell enablement, and account deletion's password rule — these are sign-in-methods' area; only the two places they COLLIDE with token clients are kept here (plugin registration order, and the named refusal when a cookie-carried challenge cannot be completed by a token-only client)

### Reference evidence

- Framework-free client auth helper: password sign-in/sign-up, device-authorization start, a SINGLE-SHOT token exchange, a polling loop, and best-effort remote sign-out, all over injectable `fetch` — `packages/core/src/session-auth.ts:1-395`
- Client-id union, the client header constant, and the response header the token arrives on — `packages/core/src/session-auth.ts:26-43`
- Token-bearing sign-in treats a missing token header as an error, not a silent cookie-only success — `packages/core/src/session-auth.ts:176-182`
- Device-grant response carries the token as `access_token` with no header; header is checked first only for forward compatibility — `packages/core/src/session-auth.ts:308-330`
- RFC 8628 retry semantics: `authorization_pending` and `slow_down` are non-terminal, everything else throws with the RFC's own code — `packages/core/src/session-auth.ts:332-339`
- Server registers the bearer plugin, with `requireSignature` deliberately off because the device grant returns a raw token while password sign-in returns a signed one — `packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:264-279`
- Second-factor plugin is registered BEFORE the bearer plugin; after-hooks run in registration order — `packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:257-263`
- Device-authorization plugin config: 10m expiry, 5s interval, `verificationUri` pointed at the FRONTEND origin, `validateClient` against an allowlist — `packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:313-329`
- Allowlist of device-flow client ids, mapped to client kinds; explicit comment that the kind is self-reported and must never be an authorization input — `packages/server/src/auth/client-label.ts:1-18`
- Session `additionalFields` `client`, `clientVersion`, `clientApiLevel`, all `input: false` so a caller cannot write them — `packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:231-263`
- Session create hook stamps the client kind and writes the client-specific expiry; update hook rewrites expiry on refresh only — `packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:377-411`
- Two session windows (30d token clients, 7d browser), with the argument for why the GLOBAL must be the long one — the refresh trigger is computed against the global — `packages/server/src/auth/session-lifetime.ts:1-127`
- Refresh reads the client STAMPED on the session row, never the triggering request, so a socket re-check cannot demote and a browser cannot promote itself — `packages/server/src/auth/session-hooks.ts:87-115`
- Refresh hook returns null unless the update is already moving the expiry, so unrelated session writes are not session extensions — `packages/server/src/auth/session-hooks.ts:125-142`
- New-session client kind falls back from the header to the `client_id` in the device-grant body — `packages/server/src/auth/session-hooks.ts:73-85`
- Devices list + revoke + revoke-others router; the session token is resolved server-side from a client-facing id and never crosses the wire — `packages/server/src/trpc/routers/devices.ts:1-127`
- Revocation is scoped to the caller's own session list, so another user's id is indistinguishable from a nonexistent one — `packages/server/src/trpc/routers/devices.ts:93-113`
- Socket upgrade token extraction from three sources in descending safety: Authorization header, `bearer.<token>` subprotocol, `?token=` last — `packages/server/src/ws/auth.ts:6-49`
- Upgrade keeps the exact headers that authenticated it, so the same lookup is repeatable for the socket's lifetime — `packages/server/src/ws/auth.ts:51-101`
- Socket re-check passes `disableCookieCache` and `disableRefresh`; a thrown lookup is `unknown`, never `revoked` — `packages/server/src/ws/auth.ts:103-139`
- The registry of watched sockets and the sweep; a probe that throws leaves the socket alive — `packages/server/src/ws/session-watch.ts:1-97`
- The revoked close code lives in the shared protocol package so both ends import one definition — `packages/shared/src/protocol.ts:193`
- Sync client latches permanently on the revoked close code, stops reconnecting, and calls `onSessionRevoked` once per client instance — `packages/core/src/sync-client.ts:35-54,144,256-266`
- Revoked handler: count durable queued work first, forget the credential, notify, redirect; the queue is deliberately kept — `packages/client/src/lib/session-revoked.ts:1-148`
- Before signing out, ask (with the cookie cache off) whether the credential still has a live session — the second-factor toggle replaces the session and would otherwise sign the user out of every tab — `packages/client/src/lib/session-revoked.ts:55-69,94-101`
- The one place the revoked handler's collaborators are the real cookie store, secret store and toast layer — `packages/client/src/lib/revoke-this-device.ts:1-59`
- Token shells' credential module: secret-store abstraction per shell, hydration gate published as a store rather than gating the React tree, 5s hydration deadline degrading to signed-out — `packages/client/src/lib/native-session.ts:1-311`
- Only ever write a truthy token — the header is emitted only when the response carries a session cookie, so an unconditional write erases a good token — `packages/client/src/lib/native-session.ts:268-288`
- Fresh-install detection: a marker in the store that IS wiped on uninstall, because keychain items outlive the app — `packages/client/src/lib/native-session.ts:181-200`
- Secret-store cloud sync explicitly disabled — a session credential must not ride to the user's other devices — `packages/client/src/lib/native-session.ts:144-152`
- Auth client captures the token off the response header and switches to `credentials: "omit"` when a token exists — `packages/client/src/lib/auth-client.ts:81-151`
- Desktop secret store: encrypted file, atomic 0600 write, and a refusal to persist when the platform backend is obfuscation rather than encryption; status is surfaced in the UI — `electron/src/secure-store.ts:1-143`
- Undecryptable ciphertext (new keychain, profile copied to another machine) is deleted rather than retried forever — `electron/src/secure-store.ts:76-86`
- Extension token lives in a memory-only, session-scoped storage area with an explicit "do not move this to local storage" rule, plus a `source` discriminator for how the session came to be — `packages/extension/src/lib/session.ts:1-90`
- Pending device authorization persisted in session-scoped storage because the worker is stopped after ~30 idle seconds; stores request id, device code, issuing origin, purpose, expected user, expiry and interval — `packages/extension/src/lib/device-auth-store.ts:1-131`
- A half-written pending record decodes to null; a web-linked record is only adopted for the user it names — `packages/extension/src/lib/device-auth-store.ts:66-111`
- Every device-code exchange runs through a serial chain so two wake-ups can never spend one code twice — `packages/extension/src/background/device-sign-in.ts:59-75`
- A freshly issued device token is resolved to its user before adoption, because the grant response names nobody — `packages/extension/src/background/device-sign-in.ts:94-127`
- The approval URL is opened only when it is https, or http on loopback — `packages/extension/src/background/device-sign-in.ts:129-138`
- Launcher client binds the stored token to the origin that ISSUED it; a token from another server reads as signed out and is never sent — `packages/raycast/src/lib/auth.ts:20-75`
- Sign-out revokes remotely against the issuing origin, then removes every local key; the remote call is best effort — `packages/raycast/src/lib/auth.ts:97-135`
- Device-approval page logic: claim the code for this session before approve/deny, with idempotent handling when a second tab won the race — `packages/client/src/lib/device-approve.ts:1-112`
- The approval page sits under the protected route prefix so an anonymous visitor signs in first — `packages/client/src/app/app/device/page.tsx:1-24`
- Origin trust for browser-hosted token clients: a per-scheme rule for random per-install origins, narrowed by "no session cookie on the request" and "never allow-credentials", behind an opt-in env switch — `packages/server/src/auth/extension-origins.ts:1-110`
- The cookie rule keys on the session cookie by name suffix, never on "any cookie", because an upgrade cannot opt out of cookies — `packages/server/src/auth/extension-origins.ts:50-72`
- CORS exposes the token response header, or a cross-origin client cannot read it at all — `packages/server/src/app.ts:56,74`
- Version/api-level headers stamped on the session row and moved forward through the adapter — `packages/server/src/auth/client-version.ts:1-60; packages/shared/src/api-level.ts:107-191; packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:413-424`
- Post-sign-in redirect allowlist explicitly includes the device-approval path, so a prefilled code survives the sign-in detour — `CLAUDE.md:1305-1316`
- Tests that pin this area against the real library: plugin order, lifetimes, socket handshake spoofs, socket revocation, upgrade probe, device flow, client labels — `packages/server/src/tests/two-factor-integration.test.ts; packages/server/src/tests/session-lifetime-integration.test.ts; packages/server/src/tests/ws-session-watch.test.ts; packages/server/src/tests/ws-revocation.test.ts; packages/server/src/tests/ws-upgrade-probe.test.ts; packages/server/src/tests/ws-room-authorization.test.ts; packages/core/src/tests/session-auth-device.test.ts`
- Headless launches must not touch the host secret store or open the OS browser; a headless run uses a separate profile because the mock key cannot decrypt the real one — `CLAUDE.md:282-289`
- Starter registers better-auth with NO plugins — no bearer, no device authorization — `starter/packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:23-75 (hatchkit worktree)`
- Starter socket upgrade is cookie-only, with no token extraction and no re-check — `starter/packages/server/src/ws/auth.ts:9-19 (hatchkit worktree)`
- Starter auth client is a bare createAuthClient: no token capture, no secret store, no credentials omission — `starter/packages/client/src/lib/auth-client.ts:50-55 (hatchkit worktree)`
- Starter has no devices router and no shared framework-free runtime package (only client/server/shared) — `starter/packages/server/src/trpc/routers/ (billing, health, items, ml, profile); starter/packages/ (hatchkit worktree)`
- Starter CORS sets no exposedHeaders, so a token header would be unreadable cross-origin — `starter/packages/server/src/app.ts:24 (hatchkit worktree)`

<a id="sign-in-methods"></a>

## Sign-in methods

**Key:** `sign-in-methods` · **Starter today:** partial · **Open rows:** 20

The reference offers exactly two ways in — email+password and one redirect-based OAuth provider — plus a second factor (TOTP with single-use backup codes), email verification, change-email, change-password and an out-of-band admin reset. Every one of those is conditional on server configuration that the server reports over an unauthenticated endpoint, so the sign-in UI can enable or disable each method truthfully in a self-hosted deployment, and the whole configuration is exercised against the real auth library in an integration test that also pins plugin registration order by reading the source. The starter has email+password sign-up/sign-in/forgot/reset pages and an OAuth provider block on the server, but the provider is never surfaced in any UI and never advertised to the client; email verification is wired into the block the library never reads and is hard-disabled; there is no second factor, no account-linking awareness, no refusal-code mapping, and no validated post-sign-in return path. On the CLI side there is no feature flag, template or prompt for any sign-in method — `Feature` covers websocket/stripe/analytics/s3/desktop/desktop-tauri/mobile only.

### Gaps

- [ ] **Report the enabled sign-in methods from the server, unauthenticated**
  - Add a config resolver that turns provider credentials and mail-transport presence into a small set of booleans, and return them from the public health/config query the sign-in screens already call. The sign-in and sign-up pages read it before anyone is signed in and enable each affordance accordingly.
  - *Invariant:* A sign-in affordance must be decided from server-reported configuration, never from a build-time constant or the mere presence of a client-side component. The same bundle is deployed to instances with different credentials configured, so a hard-coded button dead-ends at the provider and the failure is only ever seen in production. Return booleans only — never the credential values themselves.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/auth/account-security.ts:46-62 and packages/server/src/trpc/routers/health.ts:8-31`
- [ ] **Render the third-party sign-in button in every build and decide after mount**
  - One availability function with four states — not mounted, not configured on this server, unsupported on this host, enabled — rendered identically in the prerendered HTML (disabled) and resolved in an effect. Unsupported hosts get the button disabled plus a one-line note saying why, not a hidden button.
  - *Invariant:* Under a static export every page is prerendered in a Node process where neither host globals nor server configuration exist, so any render-time branch on them makes the first client render disagree with the served HTML. And a redirect-based provider returns to an http(s) origin, which a custom-scheme or file-backed shell can never be — so the flow must be refused up front with a reason rather than started and lost.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/components/google-sign-in-button.tsx:22-32,44-101`
- [ ] **Put the verification sender in the block the library reads, and require verification only when mail can be delivered**
  - Move the verification sender out of the password block into the library's dedicated verification block, and make the 'verification required' flag a function of whether any mail transport is configured. The starter has the sender under the password block, where it is never invoked, and the flag hard-coded to false.
  - *Invariant:* The verification sender is read from the verification block only; under the password block it is silently never called, so turning verification on later mails nothing and locks every new account out. Conversely, requiring verification unconditionally locks out every account on a deployment that has no transport at all — the link would only ever reach the server log.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:96-107,140-152 and packages/server/src/auth/account-security.ts:95-125 (starter: starter/packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:30-56)`
- [ ] **Never pass a callback URL to the sign-in call; request the verification link from the screen instead**
  - Leave the sign-in call's callback URL unset. On the 'address not confirmed' refusal, have the sign-in screen request a fresh verification link itself, pointed at the web origin and carrying the return path. Keep 'send on sign-in' off and 'sign in automatically after confirming' off.
  - *Invariant:* When the sign-in call is given a callback URL the response carries a redirect instruction and the auth client reloads the browser onto it, so the app's own post-auth navigation never runs and the session looks lost. And a mailed link is not a second factor: following it must not create a session on its own.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/app/login/page.tsx:80-100 and packages/server/src/auth/account-security.ts:105-122`
- [ ] **Build every mailed and OAuth callback URL absolute, from the web origin**
  - A helper that prefixes a path with the current web origin, used for every verification, reset, change-address and provider callback URL.
  - *Invariant:* The API and the UI are different origins in split deployments and in local development, so a relative callback path resolves against the API host and lands on its 404. The link has already been mailed by the time anyone notices.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/lib/auth-client.ts:341-345`
- [ ] **Log the auth link when delivery is unconfigured AND when a configured send throws**
  - Two call sites per send point: log and return when no transport is configured, and log then rethrow when a configured transport fails. Branch on a single 'any transport configured' predicate, never on one provider's variables.
  - *Invariant:* The logged link is the documented way back into an instance whose operator locked themselves out, and the case that needs it most is a transport that is configured but broken — so the log must happen before the rethrow. A check shaped around one provider's variables silently reports 'no mail' on an instance that has a different transport set up correctly, leaving the user waiting for mail nobody tried to send.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:37-49,108-126 and packages/server/src/services/email.ts:30-57,86 (starter: starter/packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:32-35,44-47)`
- [ ] **Second factor: time-based codes plus single-use backup codes, registered before the token-issuing plugin**
  - Register the second-factor plugin with an issuer label and encrypted single-use backup codes, and place it ahead of the bearer/token plugin in the plugin array. A test reads the source file and asserts the relative order.
  - *Invariant:* Plugin after-hooks run in registration order. Password sign-in creates a real session that the second-factor hook then deletes; if the token-issuing plugin runs first it emits a session-token header for that about-to-be-deleted session, and every non-cookie client stores a credential that answers 401 everywhere. Nothing about that failure points back at plugin order, which is why it is pinned by reading the source rather than by comment.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:268-289 and packages/server/src/auth/account-security.ts:12-20`
- [ ] **Enrolment order: store the secret, flip the flag only after a code verifies, show backup codes once**
  - Three steps — confirm with the password, show the enrolment URI/QR and require a generated code, then display the backup codes. The account-level flag turns on only at step three's success.
  - *Invariant:* If the flag flips when the secret is stored, a dialog closed halfway locks the account out of every future sign-in with a secret nobody ever scanned. Backup codes are stored encrypted and can never be redisplayed, so the one-time display is the only copy; a flow that shows them before the verification step shows codes for a factor that may never activate.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/components/settings/two-factor.tsx:56-70 and packages/server/src/auth/account-security.ts:63-76`
- [ ] **Name the refusal for clients that cannot carry the challenge cookie**
  - The challenge is a signed cookie and the verify endpoints read nothing else. The shared client helper detects the challenge-instead-of-session response and throws a named code; each cookie-less host shows that as 'finish this in a browser', pointing at whichever browser-approval path exists.
  - *Invariant:* A webview or extension fetch can neither read the challenge cookie nor send it back, so a code input rendered there can never succeed — it answers 'invalid code' forever, which reads as a broken second factor rather than an unsupported host. Naming the refusal is also what makes the limitation testable and documentable instead of a support mystery.
  - *Reference:* `packages/core/src/session-auth.ts:46,166-174 and packages/client/src/app/login/page.tsx:103-120`
- [ ] **Treat an expired challenge as a different outcome from a wrong code**
  - The challenge has a lifetime. Its expiry refusal code returns the user to the first step with an explanatory message and a cleared password field; every other refusal stays on the code field.
  - *Invariant:* Once the challenge has expired no code will ever be accepted, so rendering that refusal as 'wrong code' traps the user in a form that cannot succeed. The two outcomes must be distinguishable to the caller, which means the screen branches on the code, not on a boolean.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/components/two-factor-challenge.tsx:20-26,49-60`
- [ ] **Map auth refusals from the error code, never from the library's message**
  - One mapping function from refusal code to the app's own message, used by every auth form, with a per-form fallback for unmapped codes and a dedicated branch for the rate-limit status. The starter renders the library's message string directly.
  - *Invariant:* The library's message is English prose written by the library — rendering it leaks a foreign-language sentence onto a translated screen and couples the UI to internals that change between versions. An unmapped code must fall back to the form's own generic refusal, not to that text; and the same code can mean different things per form (an invalid token on a reset form means 'this link expired').
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/lib/auth-error-message.ts:1-50 (starter: starter/packages/client/src/app/login/page.tsx:22-23)`
- [ ] **Know whether an account has a password credential, and gate password-confirmed controls on it**
  - A helper that reads the account's linked credentials and answers whether a password credential exists; every control that confirms with a password (enable second factor, change password, delete account) is disabled with an explanatory description when it does not.
  - *Invariant:* An account created through a redirect provider has no password, so a password prompt there can only ever be refused. An unknown answer must default to 'has a password': asking for one that is not needed costs a retry, while failing to ask for one that is needed produces a refusal with nowhere to type the answer.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/lib/auth-client.ts:274-282, packages/server/src/auth/account-deletion.ts:73-79, packages/client/src/components/settings/two-factor.tsx:83,106`
- [ ] **Credential changes must not revoke the caller's own session**
  - Change the password without the library's revoke-other-sessions flag, then issue a separate revoke-others request when the user asked for it. Any handler that reacts to 'this session was revoked' must re-read the session with the cache disabled before signing the user out, and rebuild live connections instead when the session is still valid.
  - *Invariant:* The library's revoke-others flag deletes the current session too and issues a new one, and the server's revocation sweep runs before the response (and its rotated cookie) reaches the browser — so the device that changed its own password sees itself revoked and signs out every tab. Toggling the second factor creates a new session the same way, so any host that stores a session credential must accept the freshly issued one.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/components/settings/account-credentials.tsx:58-70,100-125 and CLAUDE.md:1115-1127`
- [ ] **Confirm an address change at the new address, and tell the truth when there is no transport**
  - The change-address call mails the confirmation link to the NEW address and the change takes effect only when that link is opened. The success dialog has two texts: 'link sent to <address>' and, when the server reports no transport, 'link written to the server log'.
  - *Invariant:* Confirming at the new address is what stops a typo from moving the account to an inbox nobody reads — an address change confirmed at the old address is unrecoverable. And a dialog that claims mail was sent on an instance with no transport sends the user to wait for a message that was never attempted.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/components/settings/account-credentials.tsx:228-245,305-312 and packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:155-161`
- [ ] **Ship a one-time backfill for accounts created before verification was switched on**
  - A script that marks accounts created before a cutoff as confirmed, counting first and updating by filter, with the cutoff defaulting to now and documented in the deploy runbook as 'run once after the deploy that configures mail'.
  - *Invariant:* Switching verification on retroactively refuses sign-in for every account that predates it — their stored flag is false and the first deploy with mail configured mails every existing user a link instead of letting them in. The backfill must be idempotent, must never un-confirm, and must take a cutoff so accounts created after the deploy still confirm themselves.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/scripts/backfill-email-verified.ts:1-52`
- [ ] **Provide an out-of-band password reset for a locked-out operator**
  - An admin command that writes the verification row the reset endpoint expects (same identifier shape, short expiry), redeems it immediately through the library's own reset endpoint, deletes the row if the redemption is refused, and then deletes every session for that account.
  - *Invariant:* The reset endpoint takes a token and the only API that mints one mails a link, so a shortcut that writes the password hash directly would bypass the length rules, the hashing, and the creation of a password credential for an account that never had one. Deleting the row on refusal matters because the endpoint only deletes it on success, leaving a live reset token behind. Sessions must be deleted explicitly unless the library is configured to revoke on reset.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/cli/accounts.ts:190-244`
- [ ] **Validate the post-sign-in return path against an allowlist**
  - One function that accepts a return path only when it starts with exactly one slash, contains no backslash, whitespace or control character, still resolves to this origin after parsing, and sits under a short allowlist of prefixes; every sign-in, sign-up and protected-layout redirect goes through it. The starter's sign-in page has no return-path handling at all.
  - *Invariant:* The sign-in page is the page everyone trusts, so an unvalidated return parameter is a ready-made phishing redirect. Each rule covers a real bypass: a doubled slash is a protocol-relative URL to another host, browsers strip tabs and newlines so a whitespace-carrying value becomes one after the check, and re-parsing normalises dot segments. Never put the account's address in a confirmation callback — the link already identifies it.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/lib/safe-next.ts:1-60 (starter: starter/packages/client/src/app/login/page.tsx has no equivalent)`
- [ ] **Exercise the sign-in configuration against the real auth library**
  - An integration test that builds an instance from the same exported option factories the app registers, on the library's in-memory adapter, computes a real one-time code the way an authenticator would, and asserts: a password alone yields no session and no token for a protected account; a valid code completes it; a backup code works exactly once; the challenge cannot be completed without the cookie but can be with it forwarded by hand; and the source file's plugin order is what it must be.
  - *Invariant:* Every rule in this area is library behaviour, not application logic, so a test against a mock pins nothing — it would pass with the plugins in the wrong order. The factories must therefore take every environment-dependent answer as an argument, so the test can build the real instance with no server environment at all.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/tests/two-factor-integration.test.ts:1-70,323-330`
- [ ] **Document each sign-in method's operational requirements at scaffold time**
  - Generated operator docs stating, per method: which environment variables enable it, the exact callback URL to register with the provider, that it is disabled on non-web hosts and why, whether registration is open and that it can only be closed at the proxy, and where the recovery link appears when no transport is configured.
  - *Invariant:* A redirect-based provider fails entirely on the provider's own error page when the registered callback URL does not match to the character, with no signal on the app side — so the exact URL must be written down where the person configuring it will read it, not derived by them.
  - *Reference:* `docs/self-hosting.md:818-900`
- [ ] **Make the set of sign-in methods a scaffold-time selection**
  - No CLI feature flag, prompt, template or update path exists for any authentication method; the feature union covers transport/payments/analytics/storage/shell wrappers only. The starter meanwhile carries a provider block that is configured from environment variables but reachable from no UI.
  - *Invariant:* A provider block with credentials and no surface, and a surface with no configuration probe, both look like working features until someone tries to sign in. If method selection is not a scaffold-time decision, the choice gets made by whoever edits the auth file later, without the matching UI, env keys, docs and tests.
  - *Reference:* `cli/src/prompts.ts:82-89 and starter/packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:58-67`

### Identifiers this area freezes

Every name below becomes a contract the first time this area ships. All of
them must come from the manifest's `identifiers` block — see
`cli/src/scaffold/identifiers.ts`. An area that spells one of these out as a
literal has re-introduced the divergence that block exists to prevent.

- The second-factor issuer label shown beside the code in every authenticator app — it is written into the enrolment URI and copied into the app at enrolment, so renaming it later leaves every already-enrolled account labelled with the old product name forever; the only fix is re-enrolment (packages/server/src/auth/account-security.ts:43)
- The auth mount path and the provider callback path under it — the callback URL is registered character-for-character in the provider's own console, so moving the mount path invalidates every registered redirect URI (docs/self-hosting.md:818-900)
- The auth cookie name prefix — the session cookie and the challenge cookie are read by literal name in origin-trust checks, error-report scrubbers and tests, so changing the prefix silently disables all three (packages/server/src/auth/extension-origins.ts:63, packages/client/src/lib/error-reporting/scrub.test.ts:100)
- Refusal codes shared between the server, the shared client library and every client surface (challenge-unsupported, deletion-needs-password, address-not-confirmed, challenge-expired, and the session-revoked socket close code) — a client compiled against an older build still compares these strings/numbers
- The client-identifier header name and the allowlisted client-id strings that a sign-in stamps onto the session record — stored sessions keep the old value after a rename, so device lists and per-client session windows misread them
- The post-authentication landing path and the allowlist of permitted return-path prefixes — both are baked into links already mailed and into links other surfaces construct
- The web origin used to build mailed callback URLs — links already delivered point at whatever it was at send time
- The verification-row identifier shape the reset endpoint expects, reproduced by the admin reset command (packages/server/src/cli/accounts.ts:223)

### Domain-specific — do not extract

- The literal issuer string "Track Your Time" and the client-id literals (…-extension, …-raycast, …-desktop) — these are naming, resolved by the naming/identifier templating work, not by this area
- Everything the account-deletion cascade does to workspace-scoped records (entries, catalog, favorites, invoices, import batches) and the owner-promotion rules — multi-tenancy domain, and a separate area
- "The running timer mirror is cleared on deletion" — the generic form is 'the host clears its single authoritative session-scoped mirror', which belongs to the client-core/offline area, not here
- The specific return-path allowlist entries (/app/track, /app/members, /invite) — the mechanism is an allowlist of prefixes; the entries are product screens
- Workspace invitation acceptance rules (invited address must match, possession of a high-entropy id is the proof) — they sit next to sign-in in the reference but are multi-tenancy
- Reference-specific operational copy in docs/self-hosting.md about which client image to use and the Caddy block that closes registration — the generic gap is 'document per-method requirements and whether registration can be closed'
- The per-provider transport names (SMTP, Listmonk) — the generic mechanism is a single 'any transport configured' predicate feeding both the verification requirement and the link-logging fallback

### Reference evidence

- Auth instance wires password sign-in, conditional verification requirement, a verification block separate from the password block, change-email, a conditional OAuth provider map, and a plugin array whose order is commented as load-bearing — `packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:96-107,140-162,203-212,268-289`
- Extracted, environment-free option factories for the second factor, the verification block and the revocation sweep, so the same objects can be handed to a test instance; also the issuer constant and the enable-only-after-verify rationale — `packages/server/src/auth/account-security.ts:12-40,43,63-76,95-125`
- resolveAuthConfig turns provider credentials and mail-transport presence into two booleans for the sign-in screens — `packages/server/src/auth/account-security.ts:46-62`
- Those two booleans are returned from the unauthenticated health query, explicitly marked public so signed-out pages can read them — `packages/server/src/trpc/routers/health.ts:8-31`
- Four-state availability function for the third-party sign-in button (pending/unconfigured/shell/enabled), decided after mount, with a per-host explanatory note — `packages/client/src/components/google-sign-in-button.tsx:22-32,44-101`
- Sign-in page: never passes a callback URL to the sign-in call, self-requests a verification link on the not-verified refusal, and branches into a second-factor step or a named refusal depending on host capability — `packages/client/src/app/login/page.tsx:80-120`
- Sign-up page detects 'account created but no session' by a null token and shows a check-your-mail screen instead of navigating — `packages/client/src/app/signup/page.tsx:60-72`
- Second-factor challenge UI: two methods (generated code, one-shot backup code), and an expired-challenge code routed back to step one rather than shown as a wrong code — `packages/client/src/components/two-factor-challenge.tsx:20-26,49-60`
- Enrolment dialog: password, then scan, then verify, then codes shown once; the row is disabled with an explanation for accounts that have no password credential — `packages/client/src/components/settings/two-factor.tsx:56-70,83,106`
- Change-password does NOT use the library's revoke-others flag, then issues a separate revoke-others request; rationale for why the flag signs the caller out — `packages/client/src/components/settings/account-credentials.tsx:58-70,100-125`
- Change-email sends the confirmation link to the NEW address and says so; when no transport is configured the dialog says the link went to the server log — `packages/client/src/components/settings/account-credentials.tsx:228-245,305-312`
- Refusal messages mapped from the library's error CODE with a documented ban on rendering the library's own message string, plus a 429 branch — `packages/client/src/lib/auth-error-message.ts:1-50`
- accountHasPassword reads the linked-accounts list and defaults an unknown answer to true — `packages/client/src/lib/auth-client.ts:274-282`
- Server-side equivalent: does this user own a password credential row — `packages/server/src/auth/account-deletion.ts:73-79`
- Shared client library names the refusal when a password sign-in is answered with a challenge it cannot carry — `packages/core/src/session-auth.ts:46,166-174`
- Callback URLs for mail and OAuth are built absolute from the web origin — `packages/client/src/lib/auth-client.ts:341-345`
- Post-sign-in return path validated against an allowlist of prefixes with same-origin re-parsing and a character blocklist — `packages/client/src/lib/safe-next.ts:1-60`
- Auth link logged whenever no transport is configured, and also logged before rethrowing when a configured transport fails — `packages/server/src/auth/auth.ts:37-49,108-126`
- Transport presence answered by one 'any transport configured' predicate rather than one provider's variables — `packages/server/src/services/email.ts:30-57,86`
- Idempotent one-time backfill marking pre-existing accounts verified, with a cutoff argument and a count-first read — `packages/server/src/scripts/backfill-email-verified.ts:1-52`
- Admin out-of-band password reset: writes the verification row the reset endpoint expects, redeems it immediately, deletes it on refusal, then deletes every session — `packages/server/src/cli/accounts.ts:190-244`
- Integration test builds an instance from the same exported factories on an in-memory adapter, computes a real one-time code, pins one-shot backup codes and the cookie-only challenge, and reads auth.ts to assert plugin registration order — `packages/server/src/tests/two-factor-integration.test.ts:1-70,323-330`
- Operator documentation: which env pair enables the provider, the exact redirect URI to register, that it is disabled in non-web shells, that sign-up is open and can only be closed at the proxy, and the lockout recovery path — `docs/self-hosting.md:818-900`
- CLAUDE.md states the five sign-in invariants with the failure each one prevents — `CLAUDE.md:1072-1137`
