<a id="multi-tenancy"></a>

## Multi-tenancy

**Key:** `multi-tenancy` · **Starter today:** absent · **Open rows:** 24

The reference has a complete multi-tenant substrate: every user gets a personal tenant at signup (repaired lazily on first read), every domain document carries a tenant id, and exactly one tRPC middleware (`workspaceProcedure`) is the only sanctioned way to obtain it — backed by a source-level test that fails the build when a router forgets. Membership is a two-record design (the auth library's `member` row plus an app-owned mirror that is the sole authorization source), with a pure-function permission matrix, ordered writes so a crash always leaves LESS access, never-zero-owners transfer, CSPRNG invitations, per-recipient realtime fan-out, per-tenant client caches, and a tenant-bound API credential. The Hatchkit starter has none of this: `starter/packages/server/src/trpc/trpc.ts` stops at `protectedProcedure`, and `starter/packages/server/src/trpc/routers/items.ts` repeats a bare `{ ownerId: ctx.user.id }` literal at every query site — precisely the pattern the reference's own comment says it removed from ~180 sites because "a resolver that forgot it did not throw; it returned somebody else's rows". Worse, `starter/packages/server/src/ws/rooms.ts` lets a client name the room it joins, which is the exact cross-account subscription hole the reference's rewritten room manager exists to close.

### Gaps

- [ ] **Introduce a tenant record and an app-owned membership mirror**
  - Add a tenant entity (the auth library's organization plugin supplies the table, sub-teams off) plus a separate app-owned membership document carrying tenant id, user id, role, display name and any per-member grant fields. Unique index on (tenantId, userId); a second index on userId for 'which tenants am I in'. The mirror is a SIBLING record, never extra columns on the library's own row, because the library owns its collection shape across upgrades while grants are business data. Every domain document gains a required tenantId field.
  - *Invariant:* The app authorizes from the mirror and from nothing else — middleware, API credentials, webhook delivery and realtime fan-out all read the same record. The moment one of them reads the auth library's row instead, the two can disagree and a removed person keeps access through whichever path still reads the stale one.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/models/WorkspaceMember.ts:1-76`
- [ ] **Add one scoping middleware and make it the only way to get a tenant id**
  - A `tenantProcedure` built on the authenticated procedure that resolves the tenant once per request and puts tenantId, the membership row and the derived grant object on the context. Every domain router uses it; nothing else is allowed to obtain a tenant id. The starter today repeats `{ ownerId: ctx.user.id }` inline at each query site.
  - *Invariant:* Scoping by a literal repeated at every query site is a convention enforced by nothing: a resolver that forgets it does not throw, it returns another tenant's rows. One middleware, or the guarantee does not exist.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/trpc/trpc.ts:94-129`
- [ ] **Resolution rule: an explicit tenant id wins and NEVER falls back; a stale session default does**
  - Read a caller-supplied tenant id off the raw input before the procedure's own schema. If present and the caller is a member, use it; if present and they are not, answer NOT_FOUND with no fallback. Only when no id was supplied may the session's stored default be used, and a default pointing at a tenant the person left falls back to their oldest membership.
  - *Invariant:* Two non-member cases, answered differently on purpose. A request that NAMED a tenant must never execute in a different one — a replayed offline write would land in the wrong tenant. A request that named none must not lock a long-lived cookie client out of everything until its session expires.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/auth/workspace.ts:75-127`
- [ ] **Read the tenant id off raw input, and force every scoped procedure to accept it**
  - A `tenantIdFromInput(raw)` helper that only reads a string key off an object, plus an optional-object scope schema that every scoped procedure composes into its input. Ship the router-walking test that fails when a scoped procedure declares no input or declares a strict object that rejects the key.
  - *Invariant:* The middleware runs before the procedure's own validation, so a procedure with no input — or a `.strict()` object — makes the explicit tenant id unreachable, silently downgrading every one of its calls to the session default. The failure is invisible until a person is in two tenants.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/tests/workspace-resolution.test.ts:149-200`
- [ ] **Auto-provision a tenant at signup and repair it on the read path**
  - A signup hook creates the person's own tenant and mirrors the owner membership. The read-path resolver ALSO creates it when a user has no membership at all. Slugs are globally unique, so derive one from the account with a stable id tail and retry with a random tail when it is taken. Creation never throws into signup.
  - *Invariant:* 'Every user has at least one tenant' has to be an invariant, not an aspiration — otherwise every query, resolver and screen needs a solo-vs-team branch. A single-user account is just a tenant of one. And because slugs are global and never freed, retrying the deterministic slug fails forever for anybody who ever left their own tenant.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/auth/personal-workspace.ts:1-150`
- [ ] **Ship the source-level guard that fails the build on an unscoped router**
  - A test that reads the router directory and asserts each file uses the scoping procedure; an explicit opt-out map where each exemption carries a written reason; a mixed-scope map naming the exact procedures allowed to run outside a tenant; a ban on the pre-tenancy owner-id literal; and a ban on `tenantId: ctx.user.id`.
  - *Invariant:* Source-level, not runtime: it needs no database, session or framework internals, so it survives upgrades and cannot be defeated by a mocking mistake. The `tenantId: ctx.user.id` ban matters because the two values coincide while every tenant is personal — the query passes every test and returns nothing the day a tenant gains a second member.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/tests/workspace-scoping.test.ts:1-161`
- [ ] **Give extracted services a scope object instead of a bare tenant id**
  - A `TenantScope = { tenantId, userId, grants }` that every service function takes. One constructor builds it from the request context, another from an authenticated API credential. No overload accepts less.
  - *Invariant:* The source-level router guard only watches the router directory; moving a resolver body into a service escapes it entirely. The scope type is what replaces the guard down there — a service cannot be called without having answered both 'which tenant' and 'what may this caller see'.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/services/scope.ts:1-48`
- [ ] **Fix the realtime room manager: rooms are derived from the authenticated identity**
  - Replace the starter's client-named rooms with a manager whose join() takes only the authenticated user id and derives the room name itself. Fan-out to a tenant is resolved in code: load the tenant's memberships and deliver per recipient. The room class holds no permission logic.
  - *Invariant:* A room is nothing but 'everyone here receives the same bytes', so a join that names its room in client input is a subscription to anybody's stream. The starter does exactly this today — `roomId` comes from a query parameter and from a client message. There must be no way to ask the manager to put a socket anywhere else.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/ws/rooms.ts:1-40`
- [ ] **Project every broadcast per recipient, re-reading memberships on each publish**
  - A publish loads the tenant's current memberships and runs a per-recipient projection: full payload to the actor, nothing at all to a recipient who may not know the record exists, a redacted copy to one with partial grants. Events that carry no actor take an explicit audience argument. An unknown event kind passes through rather than being silently dropped.
  - *Invariant:* Withholding by redaction is not enough — the mere ARRIVAL of an event tells the recipient that something happened. A recipient who may not see the record must receive nothing. And memberships must be re-read per publish, or a revocation takes effect only on process restart.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/ws/sync.ts:39-80`
- [ ] **Encode the role set and the permission matrix as pure functions with stable refusal codes**
  - Exactly three role strings in rank order, a type guard, and a defensive reader that maps anything unrecognised to the LOWEST role. Every mutation asks a pure `refuseX(actor, target, ...)` function that returns null or a stable code from a shared, exhaustive list the clients translate. Highest role reachable only by an explicit transfer, never by invitation or role edit. Grant flags follow the role: the top role's are forced on, everybody else opens CLOSED, and a role change never grants a flag.
  - *Invariant:* Some auth libraries read a comma-joined role string as the union of both roles — so an unrecognised stored value must read as the lowest role, never be trusted or split. Opening a grant must be a deliberate act; it must never be the thing that happens because a field was defaulted or forgotten. And a managing role is not the same grant as a reading role — an invited manager still starts closed.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/services/membership/permissions.ts:1-132`
- [ ] **Order the two-record membership writes so a crash always leaves LESS access**
  - Add writes the auth library's row first and the mirror last; remove deletes the mirror first, runs any tenant-local cleanup, and deletes the library row last. Every step is an idempotent update/delete by filter or an insert guarded by a read — no progress marker. Re-running the operation finishes it. A duplicate-key error on the unique index is a race that already succeeded, not a failure.
  - *Invariant:* The mirror grants access and the library row is what a retry finds the person by, so granting last and revoking first makes every intermediate state 'a library row with no mirror', which the app reads as no access. Reverse either order and a crash leaves somebody with access nobody can see or revoke.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/services/membership/lifecycle.ts:1-145`
- [ ] **Never zero owners: transfer promotes before it demotes, and the mirror goes last**
  - Ownership transfer promotes the target in BOTH records first, then steps the previous owner down — library row before the mirror, the reverse of a normal role change. Refuse handing ownership to somebody with no mirror row. The last owner cannot leave, be demoted or be removed while anybody else remains; the only member cannot leave at all.
  - *Invariant:* A crash mid-transfer must leave two owners, never none. The mirror is demoted last because the person retrying a half-finished transfer is the previous owner and the middleware reads their role from the mirror — demote it first and they become a manager who can no longer call transfer, next to a library row still claiming owner that nothing will ever finish.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/services/membership/lifecycle.ts:196-247`
- [ ] **Close the auth library's own tenancy HTTP endpoints**
  - Keep the plugin installed for its tables, its session field and its server-side creation call, but add one `before` hook that answers 404 to every one of its tenancy paths that arrived as a real HTTP request (server-side calls carry no request object and still pass). Disable tenant deletion even server-side. Ship an integration test that hits each endpoint against the real library.
  - *Invariant:* Those endpoints write only the library's own row and never the mirror, so each one is a way around every rule above: a role edit that accepts a union role, an accept/remove/leave that changes membership while the mirror says otherwise, a create-plus-set-active that points a session at a tenant with no mirror. 404 rather than 403 — the same answer an unmounted route gives, so nothing is confirmed to exist.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/services/membership/organization-lockdown.ts:1-83`
- [ ] **Foreign ids answer NOT_FOUND before any permission is consulted**
  - Every lookup of a member, invitation or tenant-owned record carries the request's tenant id in the filter. Only a real row of the caller's own tenant can earn a FORBIDDEN, and that FORBIDDEN's message is a stable code. The same rule applies to the REST surface: cross-tenant and foreign ids are 404, 403 is reserved for a genuine grant refusal on a record this tenant owns.
  - *Invariant:* A 403 confirms the id exists somewhere. Ordering the checks the other way turns every permission refusal into an existence oracle, and the shape of the id set is usually enumerable.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/services/membership/members.ts:1-40`
- [ ] **Invitations: possession of a high-entropy id plus an address match, not a verified address**
  - Invitation rows live in the auth library's own collection with its field names so existing account-deletion cleanup covers them. The id is CSPRNG (96 bits), never the storage adapter's own sequential/timestamp id. Acceptance requires the signed-in account's address to match the invited one case-insensitively, checked BEFORE the row's status, and does not require that address to be verified. TTL stated in one place and handed to the plugin too. Cap pending invitations per tenant and invitations per sender per hour (shared store when present, in-process otherwise, failing open). A re-invite refreshes and re-sends the same row. With no mail transport, or a failed send, keep the row, log the link and tell the sender to share it.
  - *Invariant:* Requiring a verified address makes invitations unusable on exactly the self-hosted deployments that have no mail to verify with, and adds nothing where mail works, because the link only ever reached that inbox. The address match — checked before the status — is what stops a forwarded link being redeemed by somebody else, and checking it first means a wrong account learns nothing about whether the link is live. An adapter-native id is a timestamp plus a counter: seeing one lets you enumerate its neighbours. Without a per-sender budget the invite form is an open mail relay, and a per-tenant pending cap alone does not close it (cancel, re-invite, repeat).
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/services/membership/invitations.ts:1-70`
- [ ] **Public acceptance route outside the protected layout, with an allowlisted return path**
  - The acceptance page lives outside the authenticated route group and reads its id from a QUERY parameter, not a path segment. Sign-in and the protected layout both carry the return path through one validator that accepts only a single-slash same-origin path under an allowlisted prefix — no backslash, whitespace or control characters. Pin the route shape in a test.
  - *Invariant:* Under the protected layout a signed-out invitee is bounced to sign-in before the page can even say whose tenant it is; and a dynamic path segment cannot be served by a statically exported client for ids that did not exist at build time. The sign-in page is the one everybody trusts, which is exactly what makes an unvalidated return parameter a phishing redirect.
  - *Reference:* `CLAUDE.md:1298-1320`
- [ ] **Per-device tenant choice — never the session's stored default**
  - Each client keeps its own chosen tenant and sends it explicitly on every request; none of them follows the session field, and only the primary web client ever writes it. Store the choice with the server origin and the account id beside it, plus the last membership list and every tenant name ever seen. Resolve the stored id against the last known list (fall back to the server's default, then the first); never send an id that failed validation. On native shells store it where the platform will not evict it, not in browser local storage.
  - *Invariant:* The session field is ONE value per session, while secondary clients hold their own sessions or piggyback on another client's — so a switch in one client would silently retarget work started from another. A stored id that no longer validates makes every scoped request answer NOT_FOUND until somebody notices. Tenant ids mean nothing on a different server, and a second account on the same machine must resolve its own default rather than inherit one.
  - *Reference:* `packages/core/src/workspace-context.ts:1-35`
- [ ] **Stamp the tenant onto outbound requests in the transport, never over an explicit value**
  - A transport link fills the device's tenant into any request input that names none. It must never overwrite one that is already there, and it must leave non-object inputs alone. For a client that starts before its stored choice has been read, use a pending marker that the request wrapper settles or strips; the marker never reaches a server.
  - *Invariant:* A replayed queued write carries the tenant it was created in; filling that in with the CURRENT tenant is precisely how work queued in one tenant lands in another.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/lib/trpc.ts:25-60`
- [ ] **Key every cache by tenant, and make switching a full page load**
  - Switching writes the choice to the shared store and then does a full navigation — never a client-side route change. Query caches, optimistic overlays and per-tenant read caches are dropped or keyed by tenant; a write that settles AFTER a switch must not apply its result or its rollback snapshot. Provide a `scopedKey(base, tenantId)` helper so no client hand-rolls the key.
  - *Invariant:* A client-side route change keeps every cached query, the live socket and any mirrored state that was built for the previous tenant's grants, so one tenant's data is drawn under the other's permissions until each happens to refetch. If cache keys do not carry the tenant, an in-flight write's answer lands in the new tenant's screens.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/components/members/enter-workspace.ts:12-39`
- [ ] **Within-tenant grants: one filter fragment, one value-level projection, shared everywhere**
  - Collapse the membership row into a grant object once, in the middleware, and thread it. Provide a `$match` fragment that restricts a query to what the caller may see (returning null when unrestricted, so callers skip the clause), and a value-level projection for surfaces that hand out a record WITHOUT re-running that query — third-party responses, webhook payloads, socket events. Both live in the shared package so they are unit-testable without a database. Withheld numeric fields are null, never zero. Combine a caller-supplied actor filter with the grant scope as two separate AND conditions.
  - *Invariant:* The query filter and the payload projection must be the same answer, or the socket comes to disagree with the API. A call site that re-derives the grant is a call site that can get it wrong. Zero is a real value; substituting it for 'withheld' makes a spreadsheet sum silently believe it. Folding the caller's filter and the grant scope into one condition is how a filter widens a scope.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/models/WorkspaceMember.ts:92-116`
- [ ] **Bind machine credentials to one tenant, with a ceiling frozen at mint time**
  - An API credential is not a session and never becomes one: it is bound to ONE tenant and to a grant ceiling fixed when it was minted, intersected with the owner's LIVE grants on every request. The third-party surface builds its scope directly from the credential and never enters the request-scoped middleware. Revoking a grant narrows the credential on the next request; granting one never widens a credential minted before. A credential belonging to a removed member is dead (unauthenticated, not forbidden).
  - *Invariant:* Keeping the two credential types separate is what makes 'a credential can never see more than its owner' a property of the type rather than of a code review — and routing the third-party surface around the request middleware is what makes 'a credential can never name a tenant of its own' structural rather than a rule repeated per handler.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/auth/api-token.ts:1-20`
- [ ] **Account deletion must branch on whether the tenant is shared**
  - Deleting an account deletes a tenant the person is alone in with everything scoped to it; in a shared tenant it deletes only their own rows and leaves tenant-owned data behind. Membership is read from BOTH records. The cascade is idempotent (every step a delete or update by filter) and the rows that locate a tenant go LAST. A shared tenant is always left with a named owner — an existing one, else the longest-standing manager, else the longest-standing member — written to both records even when no promotion is needed. Run the cascade a second time after the account row is gone.
  - *Invariant:* Reading only one membership record would call a shared tenant solo and delete colleagues' work. Removing the locating rows first strands everything inside the tenant with nothing left to find it by. Naming an owner even when no promotion is needed is what lets a retry finish a promotion a crash cut in half. And another device can recreate an auto-provisioned tenant between the two passes.
  - *Reference:* `CLAUDE.md:1144-1188`
- [ ] **Test the tenancy rules against a real multi-member fixture, not canned rows**
  - An in-memory row store seeded with a tenant of several people in different roles, driven through the REAL routers and services, so each resolver's ACTUAL filter is evaluated. Add a lifecycle test that fails every write in turn and asserts a retry converges, and a permission-matrix test per actor/target/role combination.
  - *Invariant:* A test that returns canned rows proves the resolver was called, not that it scoped anything. Only an evaluated filter proves the scope, and only a fail-every-write run proves the ordering invariants survive a crash.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/tests/support/membership-fixture.ts`
- [ ] **Decide the invitation link and stored-key names at scaffold time**
  - The public acceptance path and its query key, the per-device tenant-choice storage keys, the realtime room-name prefix, the socket envelope type string, the role strings, the refusal-code list and the credential scheme prefix are all published or persisted the first time anyone uses the app. Resolve each from the manifest once, in one place, and assert the copies agree across the shared package, the server and every client.
  - *Invariant:* Every one of these becomes a permanent contract the moment something is stored or a link is sent: renaming a storage key orphans a device's choice and its queued work, renaming the acceptance path dead-links every invitation already in somebody's inbox, and renaming a refusal code turns a translated message into a raw string on older clients.
  - *Reference:* `packages/shared/src/members.ts:33-38`

### Identifiers this area freezes

Every name below becomes a contract the first time this area ships. All of
them must come from the manifest's `identifiers` block — see
`cli/src/scaffold/identifiers.ts`. An area that spells one of these out as a
literal has re-introduced the divergence that block exists to prevent.

- the per-device tenant-choice storage key (reference: `trackyourtime.active-workspace`, packages/shared/src/members.ts:37)
- the known-tenant-list storage key (reference: `trackyourtime.known-workspaces`, packages/client/src/lib/active-workspace.ts:47)
- the realtime room-name prefix (reference: `user:` in packages/shared/src/protocol.ts:159)
- the socket envelope type string (reference: `tt:sync`)
- the tenant-id input key sent on every request (reference: `workspaceId`)
- the session field the auth plugin stores the default tenant in (reference: `activeOrganizationId`)
- the auth plugin's collection names the mirror must coexist with (reference: `organization`, `member`, `invitation`)
- the app-owned mirror collection/model name (reference: `WorkspaceMember`)
- the exact role strings (reference: `owner|admin|member`) — stored on every membership row in both records
- the stable refusal-code strings clients translate (reference: MEMBERSHIP_REFUSALS, packages/shared/src/members.ts:122-134)
- the public invitation URL shape (reference: `${FRONTEND_URL}/invite/?id=<id>`) — baked into every email already sent
- the allowlisted return-path prefixes for the post-sign-in redirect (reference: `/invite`, `/app/...`)
- the API credential scheme prefix and layout (reference: `tt_` + 8-char prefix + `_` + 43-char secret)
- the per-tenant scoped-cache key format (reference: `<base>:<tenantId>`, packages/core/src/workspace-context.ts)
- the auto-provisioned tenant's slug shape and name template (globally unique, so it cannot be changed after the first signup)

### Domain-specific — do not extract

- The one-running-timer-per-person-across-tenants invariant and the `replaced` response that reports a timer stopped in another tenant (CLAUDE.md:1290-1293) — a product-specific choice about a singleton piece of user state, not a tenancy mechanism
- `hourlyRate` on the membership row, workspace default rates, and `projectBillableByDefault` — billing domain
- `canViewOthersTime` / `canViewOthersMoney` as named flags, and the rule that money is withheld as null rather than 0 — the generic form (a per-member grant flag plus a value-level projection with a withheld-vs-zero distinction) is captured in the grants gap; the specific flag names and the currency-survives-the-strip rule are domain
- `canUseInvoices` / `invoice-permission-required` and the rule that invoice list answers [] while get answers NOT_FOUND — invoicing domain
- Report/CSV/PDF money-column withholding and `moneyVisible: false` — reporting domain
- The catalog hierarchy (Client → Project, tasks and tags beside it) being tenant-wide while entries are per-author — domain data modelling
- 'Remove and leave keep the person's entries; only account deletion removes them' — the retention policy is a product decision about tracked work, though the generic half (removal from a tenant must not delete tenant-owned records) is in the account-deletion gap
- Idle-detection not counting a colleague's activity events (packages/core/src/workspace-context.ts isOwnActivity) — time-tracking behaviour
- Stopping the departing member's running record inside the tenant during removal — generically 'run tenant-local cleanup between the two membership deletes', which is what the gap states; the timer specifics are domain

<a id="client-core-offline"></a>

## Client core + offline

**Key:** `client-core-offline` · **Starter today:** absent · **Open rows:** 22

The reference ships a complete framework-agnostic client runtime as its own workspace package (`packages/core`, ~6.2k lines of source): an async key/value storage contract with per-host adapters, a versioned envelope for every structured local value, a durable serialized FIFO mutation queue with per-row provenance stamps (owner, server origin, tenant, API level) and a hold mechanism, a pure "optimistic overlay" that answers what the UI should draw for work the server has not seen, a shared replay classifier that turns a failed replay into retry/hold/drop identically for every client surface, and a platform-truthful network verdict feeding both the queue and React Query. The starter has none of this: `packages/` contains only `client`, `server`, `shared` — there is no core package, no queue, no overlay, no versioned storage, no replay classification. The only adjacent thing it has is `packages/client/src/mobile/durable.ts`, a localStorage→Capacitor-Preferences write mirror that keeps localStorage as the source of truth — which is the exact inversion the reference rejects for data the server has never seen. The starter's QueryClient is `new QueryClient()` with no options, its Electron shell loads the export over `file://`, and nothing anywhere stamps, versions, or quarantines a locally stored value.

### Gaps

- [ ] **Add a framework-agnostic core runtime package**
  - The workspace has only client/server/shared. Add a fourth package that holds runtime shared by every client surface (web, desktop shell, mobile shell, extension, launcher, CLI) and that imports no UI framework, no DOM-only global and no transport binding of its own. It re-exports the shared package so a consumer imports one specifier. The boundary is: shared = types, schemas and pure data rules; core = runtime that does I/O through injected interfaces.
  - *Invariant:* Behaviour more than one client surface needs must live in the shared runtime package, not in the web client. Put it in a client package and the surfaces drift: two implementations of the same rule diverge silently, and neither is tested against the other's data. The package must stay importable from a non-DOM host, so a single `window`, `document` or framework import in it breaks every headless surface at once — and only at runtime.
  - *Reference:* `packages/core/src/index.ts:1-30, ARCHITECTURE.md:53-61`
- [ ] **Define one async key/value storage contract with per-host adapters**
  - A three-method async interface (getItem/setItem/removeItem returning promises) that every host can satisfy: the browser's synchronous web storage, the native shell's OS preferences store, a desktop JSON file, a launcher's own store, and an in-memory adapter for tests and server-side rendering. The synchronous-web adapter swallows every throw. Host adapters live in the host package, never in core, so core never pulls a native dependency into a surface that must not have one.
  - *Invariant:* The contract must be async even though the browser's store is synchronous, because a host that is only reachable over a native bridge cannot be made synchronous later without rewriting every caller. And the web adapter must swallow quota and privacy-mode throws: losing one persisted row is better than crashing the surface the user is looking at — but that same silence is exactly why this adapter must never back data the server has never seen (see the durability-tier gap).
  - *Reference:* `packages/core/src/storage.ts:1-60`
- [ ] **Version every structured value written to a local store**
  - Wrap each stored structured value as `{v, data}` and read it back through a spec that declares: the version this build writes, a decoder for it, an optional reader for the pre-envelope bare value, optional readers for older versions, and a miss for anything higher. Decoding never throws. A miss is null, which for a cache means 'refetch'.
  - *Invariant:* Every locally persisted value outlives the build that wrote it — and is read by the PREVIOUS build too, when a person rolls back, installs an older build, or runs a stale unpacked copy beside a new one. Without a version stamp a reader can only guess whether the shape is one it understands, and a wrong guess becomes NaN on screen or state seeded from garbage. A higher version must be a miss, never a best-effort decode: the newer build knows what its fields mean and this one does not. Storage keys are contracts and are never renamed, so the same key holds both the enveloped and the legacy shape for as long as an old install can still be updated.
  - *Reference:* `packages/core/src/versioned-storage.ts:1-102`
- [ ] **Separate durability tiers, and make the durable tier primary rather than mirrored**
  - Three stores, chosen by what the value is: credentials in the host secret store; data the server has never seen in the OS-managed app-container store; disposable per-device state (a remembered filter, a theme) in browser storage. The starter instead mirrors browser storage into the OS store and keeps browser storage authoritative, so an eviction is still a loss. Invert it: the OS store is the source of truth for the critical tier, with browser storage only as a fallback when the plugin is absent.
  - *Invariant:* The embedded web view classifies browser storage as non-critical and reclaims it after low disk or roughly a week of app inactivity — and the storage adapter swallows every throw, so that loss is silent. A mirror does not help: on the read path the evicted browser copy answers first and the write path has already been told the value is gone. What is in the critical tier is work the user did that no server has a copy of, so the OS store must be the one that is read, not the one that is written to as a backup. Credentials must NOT go in that store — it is plain user defaults, readable from an unencrypted device backup.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/mobile/preferences-storage.ts:1-30, CLAUDE.md:578-596`
- [ ] **Hand values over once when a storage key changes address**
  - When a key moves from one backing store to another, the new store performs a one-time per-key hand-over on first use: read the old location, adopt it only if the new location is empty, then delete the old copy, then write a marker keyed per key (never one marker for all keys). The whole hand-over runs inside the same one-time initialisation every read and write awaits, so it can never interleave with a write. A failed hand-over writes no marker, so the next launch retries.
  - *Invariant:* Shipping the durable store is itself a data-loss event without this: whatever a previous build wrote sits in the old location under the same key where nothing will ever read it again. Never overwrite — a value already in the new location is the newer one. Always delete the old copy — leaving it lets a rollback to the older build replay work the newer build has already sent, duplicating the user's records. And one marker per key, not one for all: with a shared marker whichever store initialises first claims it and the other key never migrates at all.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/mobile/preferences-storage.ts:31-160, docs/mobile-app-plan.md:179-183`
- [ ] **Build a durable ordered mutation queue**
  - A FIFO of pending mutations over the storage contract: enqueue/list/size/remove/clear/flush. All access is serialized through a promise chain so two concurrent enqueues cannot clobber each other through the read-modify-write window. `flush` runs a caller-supplied runner in order, drops each row as it succeeds, and stops at the first thrown failure leaving that row and everything behind it in place. Stored as `{v, data: rows}` — a bare array still reads as v1.
  - *Invariant:* Ordering is the whole point, so a flush must never skip past a row it could not send: a later mutation applied before an earlier one lands against state that does not exist yet. Serializing access is not optional — the read-modify-write window is exactly one await wide and two writes in the same tick silently drop one. And the queue is NOT read through the generic versioned decoder, whose answer to a higher version is a miss: here a miss reads as an empty queue that the next enqueue overwrites, destroying the rows.
  - *Reference:* `packages/core/src/offline-queue.ts:377-689`
- [ ] **Quarantine an unreadable queue instead of resetting over it**
  - When the stored queue cannot be parsed, copy the raw value to a timestamped sibling key before removing the original, and warn. The timestamp keeps a second corruption from overwriting the first copy. A stored format version HIGHER than this build knows locks the queue instead: its rows are listed held, enqueue and remove throw a typed locked error, and housekeeping writes (adoption, a sign-out clear) become no-ops rather than failures.
  - *Invariant:* Resetting is still right — a wedged queue would stop every future write — but the raw value is somebody's work in a shape this build failed to read, so it is copied aside rather than overwritten. And a newer format must lock rather than reset: writing would replace rows this build cannot read with rows a newer build would then misread. A housekeeping write on a locked queue must not fail loudly — refusing it would break sign-out — and skipping it loses nothing that is this build's to keep.
  - *Reference:* `packages/core/src/offline-queue.ts:104-135, 417-460`
- [ ] **Stamp every queued row with its provenance, and adopt unstamped rows**
  - Each row carries four optional stamps written at enqueue time: the account that made it, the server origin it was made against, the tenant/scope it was made in, and the API level of the build that wrote it. Each stamp has a matching replay predicate (may this principal send it / is this its origin / is this scope still a membership) and a matching eager adoption function that claims rows written before the stamp existed. Adoption and the replay predicate must make the SAME decision about an unstamped row.
  - *Invariant:* Every stamp must stay optional forever: rows written by a build that predates it must still decode, and one of those rows is work with no other copy. The identifier is stored raw, never hashed — the check is an equality test that decides whose account a write lands in, and a short non-cryptographic hash makes it probabilistic, where one collision reintroduces exactly the cross-account replay the stamp prevents. An unstamped-origin row belongs to the build's DEFAULT origin, not the current one, or the first switch hands every legacy row to the wrong server. If adoption and the replay predicate disagree about an unstamped row, one principal adopts it and another replays it.
  - *Reference:* `packages/core/src/offline-queue.ts:7-75, 141-175, 283-395`
- [ ] **Read the scope stamp once per write, and send it explicitly on replay**
  - The request and the queued row must name the same scope: capture it before the first await of the write, not again when a hung request finally fails. On replay, set the row's stamp over anything already on the input — client-level scope injection only fills a gap and must never fill the gap a stamped row deliberately leaves. A write that settles after the user switched scope must not touch the cache, and anything cached is keyed by scope.
  - *Invariant:* Reading the scope again at failure time stamps the row with whatever the user switched to in the meantime, and that row then replays into the wrong tenant with nothing on screen to say so. Cache keys usually do not carry the scope, so a late answer or a rollback snapshot lands in the new scope's screens. These two fail completely silently — the data is plausible, just filed against the wrong owner.
  - *Reference:* `packages/core/src/offline-replay.ts:38-56, CLAUDE.md:936-952`
- [ ] **One shared classifier turns a failed replay into retry / hold / drop**
  - A single pure function, used by every surface, that maps a replay failure to: applied, retry-later (transport, unauthorized, server, membership), hold (with a reason), or drop (refused, or unaddressable-and-stale). A surface supplies only its own transport test and its own membership re-check. The ordered policy: stale unaddressed mutation drops; no answer at all keeps; unauthorized keeps; 'server has no such procedure' holds — checked BEFORE the permanent set; anything outside the permanent set keeps; a bad-request on a row of higher API level than the server holds; a not-found on a scoped row is kept unless the scope is confirmed; the rest is a refusal on the merits.
  - *Invariant:* The permanent-rejection set must exclude 401 and 5xx — discarding someone's unsent work because a token expired or a server hiccuped is far worse than a queue that waits. 403 must be IN it: in a shared scope a forbidden is a verdict on one row by a perfectly valid session, and treating it as a lost session wedges every row behind it. A status is only a verdict when the API itself gave it: a non-envelope body (a WAF page, a proxy's HTML 404, a captive portal) says nothing about the row, and dropping on it deletes work because a network was in the way. 'No such procedure' is told from an application not-found by the MESSAGE — both carry the same code and status — and checking it after the permanent set deletes a newer client's rows against an older self-hosted server.
  - *Reference:* `packages/core/src/offline-replay.ts:192-360, packages/core/src/api-client.ts:33-103`
- [ ] **Hold rows that cannot be sent yet, and never delete them on their own**
  - A row this build or this server cannot handle is held: kept, counted, listed with a reason and a deliberate named discard. A reason union with a record that declares, per reason, what ends it — a new build, or a server change (re-asked after a retry interval, or sooner on a launch or resume). A reason that depends on the READING build ('this build cannot decode the row') is never written onto the row; it is recomputed on every read, so a newer build that understands the row releases it. Holds follow chains. Held rows are excluded from every 'is something ahead of a new mutation' count.
  - *Invariant:* A held reason stored on the row would outlive the upgrade that ends it. Holds must follow the chain of rows that depend on each other, or a dependent row replayed without the row it depends on applies against the wrong target. Held rows must be excluded from the pending count, or every future write queues behind a row that never drains. And no age-based expiry: deleting somebody's unsent work on a timer is still deleting it silently — the only way out names what it is destroying.
  - *Reference:* `packages/core/src/offline-ops.ts:243-340, CLAUDE.md:2348-2384`
- [ ] **Chain dependent queued rows by a client-minted temporary id**
  - A mutation that invents a record client-side mints a temp id with a recognisable prefix; later mutations against that not-yet-real record ride the same temp id. The flush keeps a per-run map from temp id to the real id the creating mutation produced, and rewrites dependent rows' targets as they replay. The temp id is readable from a raw row WITHOUT decoding it, so a row this build cannot read still chains the rows that depend on it. Editing a record that is still temporary is refused with a typed error; discarding one drops its queued rows rather than sending them.
  - *Invariant:* A dependent mutation replayed without its creating mutation is applied against whatever happens to be current on the server — a silently wrong target. The temp-id read must not require decoding, or an undecodable row breaks the chain it anchors. An update naming a temp id is refused permanently by the server on replay and a permanent refusal is dropped, so the edit would vanish with no error anywhere — refuse it at the client instead. And discarding a temp record must drop its queued creation, or the creation resurrects it a minute later.
  - *Reference:* `packages/core/src/offline-ops.ts:34-40, packages/core/src/offline-replay.ts:86-130, CLAUDE.md:2280-2292`
- [ ] **Keep an optimistic overlay separate from the queue**
  - A pure, host-free structure holding the visible consequence of unsent work: records invented locally, patches to server-held records by id, and ids queued for removal — with the algebra for applying it over a server answer (a queued removal hides a record, a second edit builds on the first, a local record outside the requested window is not shown). Parsed row by row so one unreadable row is dropped alone. Persisted through the versioned envelope.
  - *Invariant:* The queue answers 'what still has to be sent'; only the overlay can answer 'what should this surface be showing right now'. Without it, work done with no connection is a row in storage and nothing on screen — every surface keeps drawing the last successful read. The algebra must be pure and in the shared package: these are the rules that go quietly wrong, and a host that keeps them beside its own storage code is a host where nothing can test them. An unparseable overlay must be an empty overlay, never a throw — a device that cannot read its own scratch state must still render the server's answer.
  - *Reference:* `packages/core/src/offline-overlay.ts:1-90, CLAUDE.md:2254-2266`
- [ ] **Take the network verdict from the platform, not from the browser**
  - One module owns 'are we online'. On a native shell it reads the OS reachability API; elsewhere it falls back to the browser flag. Not-yet-heard is null, never false. One subscription surface feeds three consumers: the transport-failure test, the queue's flush trigger, and the data layer's online manager. The OS listener is registered BEFORE the first status read.
  - *Invariant:* The browser's online flag routinely reports true on a dead radio inside an embedded web view and never fires for airplane mode. The transport-failure test short-circuits on this verdict, so a lying true files a genuine server refusal as a transport failure and queues it forever instead of rolling it back; a lying false makes a real refusal look like a hiccup. Null must not collapse to false, or the first milliseconds of every launch declare the app offline. Registering the listener after the first read leaves the app one state change behind, permanently.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/mobile/network.ts:1-108`
- [ ] **Opt exactly the queue-owning mutations out of the data layer's offline pause**
  - The query library pauses a mutation while it believes the device is offline: the mutation function never runs and the error handler never fires. The error handler is where work is queued. So the mutations the queue owns — and only those — run in always-send mode: the creation/update family, any grid or bulk write family, and the replay itself. It is applied per mutation, never as a global default.
  - *Invariant:* The queue IS the pause mechanism for these writes and it needs the failure to happen; with a truthful network verdict wired in, the library's default makes them do nothing at all. Making it global is the other half of the trap: it takes pause-and-resume away from the dozens of mutations that queue nothing — on desktop as much as on mobile — so they roll back and toast on a blip where they would have replayed on reconnect. The replay itself must also be always-send or a paused replay leaves the flush awaiting a promise that never settles and the queue latched as flushing for the rest of the launch.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/lib/query-client.ts:78-124, CLAUDE.md:606-622`
- [ ] **Mirror the single authoritative session-scoped state, and let only a real answer overwrite it**
  - Write every resolved server answer about the one live session-scoped record into the durable store; seed the in-memory store from that mirror at boot, before the query fires; mark the seed provisional. The consumer waits for an actual success before touching the store. Derive any elapsed/live figure from a stored start timestamp against the wall clock on every tick, never accumulate. Mirror on native shells only.
  - *Invariant:* A pending or failed read is not a statement about the state. An ungated effect reads 'no data yet' as 'nothing is active' and wipes the seed on the first render — which is precisely the cold launch with no connection the mirror exists for. Only a real success may overwrite the mirror, including with an empty answer, which is how state ended on another device disappears. Accumulating rather than deriving drifts silently across sleep, backgrounded tabs and throttled timers. Seeding on the web instead would flash the previous state on every reload.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/lib/running-mirror.ts:1-80, packages/core/src/timer-store.ts:14-40, CLAUDE.md:622-637`
- [ ] **Record what this install last did, so non-refetching surfaces are not stale**
  - A small local record of the last state transition this install performed, with a timestamp from the same clock as the fetch timestamp, in a key every surface of the install can read without a round trip. A snapshot fetched BEFORE the record loses to it; the next successful fetch carries a later timestamp and from that instant the record can no longer win.
  - *Invariant:* Surfaces that keep painting between fetches — a menu bar item, a tray icon, a widget — render a snapshot that was only ever true when it was fetched, so they keep showing state the user ended on this very machine. Expiry must be by construction (the fetch timestamp overtakes it), never a TTL: a TTL either masks a change made on another device or lets the stale record win again after a gap.
  - *Reference:* `packages/core/src/timer-echo.ts:1-60`
- [ ] **Fix the resume order, and mount the drain above the route**
  - On foreground/resume, in this order: reconnect the realtime connection; recompute derived live values synchronously; refresh the server's capability level without blocking anything but a flush; then flush the queue — and refetch the authoritative read ONLY when nothing was queued. Extract the ordering as a pure function so it is testable without a bridge, a socket or a component tree. Mount the drain at the application shell, not on one route.
  - *Invariant:* Refetching before flushing asks a server that has never heard of the work done with no connection, gets an empty answer, and blanks the live state until the flush lands — the state visibly vanishes and returns, on exactly the recovery path this exists to smooth. The realtime connection must be reconnected because the server drops it on the first missed keepalive while a frozen socket delivers no close event, so the client still believes it is open. And mounting the drain on one route means reconnecting on any other screen drains nothing — guaranteed on a device that resumes on whatever screen it was left on.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/hooks/use-native-lifecycle.ts:17-75, CLAUDE.md:626-643`
- [ ] **Do not queue a write that fails during document teardown**
  - A latch set on page-hide (ignoring back-forward-cache suspensions) and cleared on page-show; the enqueue path checks it. Web only — explicitly disabled on native shells.
  - *Invariant:* A write failing because the document is being torn down is not offline work: the app is a moment from reloading and asking the server what is actually there, which beats a blind replay — and without the latch the NEXT document replays it, duplicating the record. But a latched flag on a native shell would silently stop queueing real offline work, because there is no navigation teardown there and the same event fires for backgrounding.
  - *Reference:* `packages/client/src/lib/offline.ts:770-814, docs/mobile-app-plan.md:218-231`
- [ ] **Cache each server origin's capability level, and gate queued rows on it**
  - A per-origin cache of what each server has said about its capability level, refreshed at launch, resume, origin switch, and whenever a replay is held for a missing procedure. Used for feature gating, for holding rows the server is too old for, and for compatibility banners. An origin never asked is null. Rows are stamped with the writing build's level at enqueue; a flush holds such a row BEFORE sending it when the server's known level is lower, and releases it the moment the level is high enough.
  - *Invariant:* Never-asked must be null, never zero — zero is a real answer from a server released before the handshake existed. The cache is per origin because a client can be moved between servers whose levels have nothing to do with each other. Holding before sending matters: a server that does not know a field either strips it or refuses it, and either way the work is lost. And the hold must be decided by the reported level rather than by a clock, or it survives the upgrade that ended it.
  - *Reference:* `packages/core/src/server-level.ts:1-80, packages/core/src/offline-replay.ts:263-278`
- [ ] **Describe a queued row in one shared place before offering to destroy it**
  - A single function that renders a queued row as something a person can recognise — what it was, when the work happened (the payload's own timestamp, else when it queued), which origin and scope it belongs to, and why it is held — used by every surface that offers to discard queued work. Rows it cannot decode return a null op rather than throwing.
  - *Invariant:* Rows kept and never replayed are immortal on their own, and a permanent count nobody can act on is a scold. The only way out must NAME what it destroys: discarding '3 changes' is not a decision anybody can make. Shared across surfaces, or two clients ask the same person to approve two different deletions of the same rows.
  - *Reference:* `packages/core/src/offline-ops.ts:395-472`
- [ ] **Never serve the packaged desktop shell from file://**
  - The starter's Electron main loads the static export with `loadFile(...)`. Register a privileged custom scheme and serve the export from a fixed scheme+host origin instead, resolving paths the same way the web server does. Both halves of the origin are frozen at scaffold time.
  - *Invariant:* A file:// document's origin serialises to null: per-origin local storage is not persisted, credentialed requests are rejected outright, and sign-in fails on a missing-or-null-origin check while the first route still renders — so it looks like it works. The scheme and host are then contracts: the origin keys local storage (which is where the queue and every stamped value live) and it must appear verbatim in the server's trusted-origin allowlist. Changing either later orphans every install's stored state silently.
  - *Reference:* `starter/electron/main.ts:180 (starter), CLAUDE.md:193-199`

### Identifiers this area freezes

Every name below becomes a contract the first time this area ships. All of
them must come from the manifest's `identifiers` block — see
`cli/src/scaffold/identifiers.ts`. An area that spells one of these out as a
literal has re-introduced the divergence that block exists to prevent.

- storagePrefix — the namespace for every local storage key the client writes; concretely the queue key, the queue-owner key, the local-transition-echo key, the authoritative-state mirror key, the per-key migration markers, and the derived corrupt-copy key `<queueKey>.corrupt.<ms>` (cli/src/scaffold/identifiers.ts:93-97 already declares this field; packages/core/src/offline-ops.ts:19,31 and packages/core/src/timer-echo.ts:37 bake it in the reference)
- keychainService — service name in the host secret store (macOS Keychain, Windows Credential Manager, libsecret, extension session storage, launcher store). A secret-store item outlives the app install, so a rename strands it (cli/src/scaffold/identifiers.ts:99-102)
- npmScope — the workspace scope the core package is imported under (`@<scope>/core`, plus `@<scope>/core/<module>` subpath exports). Every client file's import specifiers depend on it (packages/core/package.json exports map)
- desktopOrigin.scheme and desktopOrigin.host — the packaged desktop shell's serving origin. It keys local storage and must appear verbatim in the server's trusted-origin allowlist (cli/src/scaffold/identifiers.ts:128-133)
- clientHeader — the lowercase request header each surface sends to identify itself; used for per-surface session lifetime and device labels (cli/src/scaffold/identifiers.ts:103-106)
- clientIds per surface — device-flow client ids; a row's replay path and the queue's server binding both assume a stable per-surface identity (cli/src/scaffold/identifiers.ts:111-113)
- QUEUE_FORMAT_VERSION and the per-value versioned-envelope version numbers — not scaffold-time names, but permanent numeric contracts the moment a build ships (packages/core/src/offline-queue.ts:110)
- the queue op names themselves (the strings stored in each row's `op` field) — a row written under one name is undecodable under another, and undecodable means held forever (packages/core/src/offline-ops.ts:113-121)

### Domain-specific — do not extract

- The concrete op set `entries.start / stop / create / update / remove / discard` and their payload shapes (description, projectId, taskId, billable, start/end, tagIds) — packages/core/src/offline-ops.ts:52-118. The TEMPLATE takes the op-registry mechanism, not these ops.
- 'The running timer' as a concept — a single live record with a start timestamp and derived elapsed seconds (packages/core/src/timer-store.ts). De-domained above as 'the single authoritative session-scoped record'; the seconds-ticking specifics are domain.
- Money: project rate, workspace currency, the 'money snapshot the server is about to write' that the local read cache exists to guess (CLAUDE.md:2258-2262) — billing domain, no generic form.
- Client / project / task / tag hierarchy and the deliberate absence of a client field on an entry (ARCHITECTURE.md:278-283) — pure product data model.
- Invoices, timesheet grid, reports, budgets, the runaway-timer guard that caps a long-running record server-side (packages/core/src/api-client.ts:52-58 cites it as the motivating case for permanent-rejection handling) — the generic lesson was extracted; the guard itself is domain.
- The idle watcher and its entry-ownership claim that must be renamed from temp id to real id after replay (packages/core/src/offline-ops.ts:352-392) — the temp-id rename is generic and captured, but 'idle detection for a running timer' has no generic template form.
- Pomodoro and quick-start (packages/core/src/pomodoro.ts, quick-start.ts) — product features.
- `STALE_STOP_MS` = 24h and the specific semantics of 'stop whatever is running' (packages/core/src/offline-replay.ts:58-84). The generic mechanism — a queued mutation that addresses 'whatever is current' rather than a specific record must be refused once it is too old to be trusted — is captured as a gap; the one-day figure and the stop semantics are domain.
- Activity segments and suggestion rules (packages/core/src/activity/*) — a product feature that happens to live in core.
- The specific tenancy vocabulary 'workspace' / 'organization' / membership roles — generalised above as 'scope' / 'tenant', but the reference's membership-list refetch procedure and role model are product-specific.

### Reference evidence

- A separate framework-free workspace package, explicitly barred from React/Next/DOM assumptions, re-exporting the shared package plus 22 runtime modules — `packages/core/src/index.ts:1-30`
- Async 3-method KeyValueStorage contract, an in-memory adapter, and a sync-localStorage adapter that swallows quota/privacy throws so a failed persist never crashes the surface — `packages/core/src/storage.ts:1-60`
- `{v,data}` envelope for structured local values, with legacy (pre-envelope) readers, older-version readers, higher-version = miss, and a doc comment stating it is NOT for data with no other copy — `packages/core/src/versioned-storage.ts:1-102`
- Durable FIFO mutation queue: serialized read-modify-write chain, corrupt-value quarantine to `<key>.corrupt.<ms>`, format-version lock that refuses writes and holds rows, and flush that stops at the first failure preserving order — `packages/core/src/offline-queue.ts:377-689`
- Per-row provenance stamps with a paragraph of rationale each: `owner` (raw id, never hashed — a collision reintroduces cross-account replay), `server`, `workspaceId`, `hold`, `apiLevel` — `packages/core/src/offline-queue.ts:7-75`
- Predicates for whose row it is: isReplayableBy / isForeignTo / isQueuedOn (with a legacy-origin argument) / isReplayableIn / isForeignWorkspace / refusalKeepsRow — `packages/core/src/offline-queue.ts:283-395`
- Adoption of unstamped legacy rows — adoptUnowned, adoptUnserved, adoptUnstampedWorkspace — each eager and each matching the corresponding replay predicate — `packages/core/src/offline-queue.ts:141-175`
- Op/payload contract shared by every client surface, temp-id minting, decode that returns null (never throws) for an op this build does not know — `packages/core/src/offline-ops.ts:1-245`
- Hold machinery: HoldReason union, HOLD_RELEASE record forcing every new reason to declare what ends it, HELD_RETRY_MS, holdReasonOf recomputed per read, holdBlocksReplay, heldReasons following temp-id chains — `packages/core/src/offline-ops.ts:243-340`
- describeQueuedMutation — one shared human-readable summary of a queued row, so two surfaces cannot ask a person to approve two different deletions — `packages/core/src/offline-ops.ts:395-472`
- Replay: workspace-stamped input set OVER any client-injected default, targeted-vs-unaddressed resolution through a per-flush temp-id→real-id map, staleness refusal — `packages/core/src/offline-replay.ts:38-190`
- classifyReplayOutcome — the single classifier, with its 8-step ordered policy documented, returning applied/retry-later/hold/drop for all clients — `packages/core/src/offline-replay.ts:192-360`
- Permanent-rejection set {400,403,404,409,410,422}, the PARSE_ERROR guard (a status is only a verdict when the API gave it), and isTransportFailure as a type test not a string test — `packages/core/src/api-client.ts:33-103`
- Pure overlay algebra — entries invented locally, patches by id, removals — parsed row-by-row so one bad row is dropped rather than the whole overlay — `packages/core/src/offline-overlay.ts:1-90`
- Per-origin server API-level cache used for feature gating, holding rows, and compatibility banners; never-asked is null, never level 0 — `packages/core/src/server-level.ts:1-80`
- Authoritative session state derived from a start timestamp against the wall clock on every tick, never accumulated, so it survives sleep and throttled timers — `packages/core/src/timer-store.ts:14-40`
- A local 'echo' of what this install last did, which beats any snapshot fetched before it and expires by construction rather than by TTL — `packages/core/src/timer-echo.ts:1-60`
- Capacitor Preferences as the PRIMARY store for durable data (not a mirror), with a per-key one-time hand-over from localStorage, never-overwrite semantics, removal of the localStorage copy to prevent rollback double-replay, and plugin handles wrapped in plain objects — `packages/client/src/mobile/preferences-storage.ts:1-244`
- Network verdict from the OS radio on native with null meaning 'not heard yet', one subscription surface, listener registered before the first status read — `packages/client/src/mobile/network.ts:1-108`
- OFFLINE_QUEUED_MUTATION (`networkMode: "always"`) applied to exactly the three mutation families that catch the failure, deliberately NOT a global default; onlineManager fed the same verdict; bounded retries on native — `packages/client/src/lib/query-client.ts:55-128`
- Authoritative-state mirror written on every server answer, seeded at boot, provisional flag, native-only — `packages/client/src/lib/running-mirror.ts:1-80`
- runResume — the ordered resume sequence (reconnect, tick, refresh server level, flush, and invalidate ONLY when nothing was queued) extracted as a pure function so the ordering is testable — `packages/client/src/hooks/use-native-lifecycle.ts:17-75`
- Document-unload latch that suppresses queueing a write failing during teardown — web only, bfcache-aware, explicitly disabled on native — `packages/client/src/lib/offline.ts:770-814`
- Host-bound half of the queue: storage selection memoised on a synchronous native probe, reactive pending count, tRPC error classification — `packages/client/src/lib/offline.ts:1-120`
- CLAUDE.md: the three-store split (credential in secret store, unsent data in OS preferences, disposable state in localStorage) with the eviction reasoning — `CLAUDE.md:578-596`
- CLAUDE.md: network truth from the radio, and why the queueing mutations must run in `networkMode: "always"` — `CLAUDE.md:596-622`
- CLAUDE.md: the mirror is only overwritten by an answer; resume order is load-bearing; the drain is mounted app-wide not per-route — `CLAUDE.md:622-643`
- CLAUDE.md: every queued row carries the server it was queued against; validate a server before saving it — `CLAUDE.md:808-825`
- CLAUDE.md: rows survive sign-out so every row records who queued it; the stamp cannot come from the session hook alone; a foreign row is neither replayed nor deleted — `CLAUDE.md:877-908`
- CLAUDE.md: tenant stamping rules — stamp read once per write, NOT_FOUND re-asks before dropping, only UNAUTHORIZED halts a flush, anything cached is keyed by tenant — `CLAUDE.md:914-950`
- CLAUDE.md: held rows and the queue format — one classifier, unknown-procedure vs application NOT_FOUND, unknown-op never written onto a row, `{v:1,data:rows}` envelope and the lock — `CLAUDE.md:2348-2392`
- CLAUDE.md: 'Never change the scheme or host — the origin keys localStorage and every server's TRUSTED_ORIGINS'; never file://, whose origin is null — `CLAUDE.md:193-199`
- ARCHITECTURE.md: the shared-vs-core boundary — shared is data and pure functions, core is runtime; putting core behaviour in a client package is how surfaces drift — `ARCHITECTURE.md:53-61`
- mobile-app-plan.md: the measured corrections — the storage swap alone was not enough without the hand-over; the networkMode change; the unload guard found only after moving the mount — `docs/mobile-app-plan.md:172-231`
