<a id="server-platform-public-api"></a>

## Server platform + public API

**Key:** `server-platform-public-api` · **Starter today:** partial · **Open rows:** 19

The reference ships a complete third-party integration platform: a versioned REST surface (`/api/v1`) generated from a single route table, scoped bearer credentials with hashed storage and a frozen-ceiling authorization model, three-layer rate limiting with a pre-authentication failure budget, RFC 9457 error bodies, opaque keyset cursors, a committed OpenAPI document plus a committed tRPC contract snapshot with breaking/additive classification, and a full outbound webhook subsystem (signed envelopes, an enqueue/sweeper queue with atomic claims, per-attempt SSRF re-resolution with address pinning, a backoff table, auto-disable, and a TTL'd delivery log). The starter has essentially none of it. What the starter does have is one INBOUND third-party webhook receiver mounted with a raw body ahead of `express.json()` (`starter/packages/server/src/app.ts:42-47`), one in-memory per-IP sliding-window limiter used by a single newsletter route (`starter/packages/server/src/services/newsletter/subscribe.ts:170-195`), and a global error handler that returns `err.message` verbatim outside production (`starter/packages/server/src/middleware/error-handler.ts:26-34`) — which is exactly the leak the reference's API layer exists to sidestep. There is no `api/v1`, no token model, no OpenAPI/spec emit, no contract snapshot, no outbound webhooks, and no `public-api` entry in the CLI's `Feature` union (`cli/src/prompts.ts:82-88`).

### Gaps

- [ ] **Make one route table the sole source of both routing and the published spec**
  - A single exported array declares every public route: method, path, required scope, summary, an input descriptor (`query` | `body` | `path` + schema) and an output schema. The HTTP router mounts from it and the spec generator reads it. Request schemas are the SAME objects the internal layer validates with; response schemas are declared once and pinned to the shared wire types via `z.ZodType<T>` so dropping a field breaks the build instead of quietly shrinking the documented response. Mounting a listed route with no registered handler throws at process start. Mount order follows table order.
  - *Invariant:* A route cannot exist undocumented (nothing is mounted that is not in the table) and cannot be documented-but-unimplemented (a missing handler is a boot failure, not a production 404). A hand-written spec beside hand-written wiring drifts inside one release and nobody notices until an integrator builds against a route that never existed. Corollary that also fails silently: a literal path segment must be registered before the parameterized sibling that would swallow it, or the literal is looked up as an id and answers 404.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/api/v1/routes-table.ts:1-14, packages/server/src/api/v1/index.ts:104-140`
- [ ] **Generate the machine-readable spec from the validating schemas, and commit it**
  - Build the OpenAPI 3.1 document from the same schemas the handlers validate with, using the schema library's own JSON-Schema emitter rather than a second annotation library. Emit two artifacts — the spec document and a human reference page — into the docs tree, commit both, and add a test that regenerates and byte-compares them. Serve the same document from a live unauthenticated route as well. Derive operation ids from method+path so regenerating a client does not rename every call site.
  - *Invariant:* A spec regenerated only at deploy time is a spec nobody reviews in a diff, so the artifacts are committed and staleness is a red test rather than a silent state. The live spec route must need no credential — a generator that requires a credential before it can see the route list is a generator nobody runs. And the schema must never be looser than the runtime: a documented format that admits values the server never sends produces a generated client that validates a shape it will never receive, and the author only finds out on a round-trip.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/api/v1/openapi.ts:1-16, packages/server/src/api/v1/emit-openapi.ts:1-13, packages/server/src/tests/openapi-document.test.ts:1-26`
- [ ] **Give the public surface its own error envelope, owned end to end**
  - A structured error body (RFC 9457 `application/problem+json`: type URI, title, status, detail, instance), a slug-per-failure taxonomy, one function mapping internal error types onto it, and a wrapper that catches everything a handler throws and converts it — never delegating to the application's global error middleware. A 5xx `detail` is one fixed string in every environment; the thrown message goes to a structured log line. Validation errors are the one class whose message is safe to return in full. The surface's own 404 fallback answers in the same media type as everything else in it.
  - *Invariant:* The starter's global error handler returns `err.message` verbatim whenever NODE_ENV is not production, so a driver or ORM message — carrying collection names, query shapes, sometimes values — becomes part of an API response the moment somebody runs the server with the env var unset. That is why this layer answers its own errors rather than calling `next(err)`. Second invariant: a client that parses one error format cannot parse a second, so the not-found fallback inside the surface must not fall through to the app's own JSON shape.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/api/v1/problem.ts:1-8, :30-36, packages/server/src/api/v1/index.ts:48-83, :142-152`
- [ ] **Fix the disclosure policy for statuses: what 401, 403 and 404 each mean**
  - One 401 body for every credential failure mode (absent, malformed, unknown, revoked, expired, no longer a member) — the distinctions go to the log only. A resource belonging to another tenant, or hidden by the caller's permissions, answers 404. 403 is reserved for a scope or permission refusal on a resource this tenant genuinely owns. Status codes an internal error type does not explicitly map to fall through to 500 rather than being guessed at.
  - *Invariant:* Telling 'no such credential' apart from 'wrong secret' turns the endpoint into an oracle that confirms which credential prefixes exist. A 403 on a foreign id confirms the id exists somewhere, which turns the endpoint into an enumeration oracle. An unmapped error code is one nobody decided the disclosure rules for, so guessing 400 leaks the distinction between 'you asked wrong' and 'we broke'.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/api/v1/auth.ts:44-61, packages/server/src/api/v1/problem.ts:52-68, :176-186`
- [ ] **Add a scoped bearer credential with a prefix/secret split and hashed storage**
  - Mint `<scheme>_<non-secret lookup prefix>_<secret>` from a CSPRNG. Store only a sha256 of the WHOLE plaintext plus the prefix (uniquely indexed). Parse a presented value by POSITION, not by splitting on the separator. Look the row up by prefix (one indexed read, never a scan) and compare in constant time after a length check. Return the plaintext exactly once, from the create call; no wire type in the codebase can carry it back out, because no column holds it. Scopes are an explicit list checked by a `hasScope`-style function; an absent list grants nothing. Throttle the `lastUsedAt` write and never let it fail a request. Cap live credentials per member.
  - *Invariant:* Five things fail quietly here. (1) Hashing only the secret half lets a leaked prefix be re-paired with a guessed secret. (2) Splitting on the separator addresses the wrong row whenever the alphabet includes that character — base64url contains `_`. (3) A `scopes ?? ALL_SCOPES` default anywhere inverts deny-by-default for every row written before the field existed, silently. (4) A write scope implying a read scope is the kind of implication a reviewer stops re-checking. (5) The per-credential rate limit is only a limit while the credential count is bounded — unbounded minting is a linear budget multiplier, i.e. no limiter at all. Also: a KDF here buys nothing (the input is CSPRNG output, there is no dictionary) and costs ~100ms on every call.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/auth/api-token.ts:20-30, :41-56, :70-110, :113-127, :157-172, packages/server/src/trpc/routers/api-tokens.ts:44-66, packages/shared/src/api-tokens.ts:11-24`
- [ ] **Resolve a long-lived credential's authority as live permissions AND a frozen mint-time ceiling**
  - Store the granting member's permission grant on the credential as a CEILING, and on every request intersect it with that member's permissions right now. A credential whose owner has lost their membership authenticates to nothing. Keep this credential type structurally distinct from the interactive session type — the session follows a person across tenants, the credential is bound to exactly one.
  - *Invariant:* Neither half alone is safe. Serving from the frozen snapshot means revoking a permission does not narrow credentials minted before it. Serving from live permissions alone means a later, unrelated grant silently widens a credential its owner minted for a narrow job. Conflating credential and session means a credential gains reach whenever its owner joins another tenant. And a departed member's credential must be 401 (nothing left to act as), not 403 — 403 would confirm the tenant exists.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/models/ApiToken.ts:56-72, packages/server/src/auth/api-token.ts:1-9, :175-186`
- [ ] **Make 'a credential cannot name its own tenant' structural, not a per-handler rule**
  - The public surface authenticates, builds a tenant scope from the credential, and calls the SAME extracted service functions the internal RPC layer calls — no synthetic internal context, no path through the tenant-resolving procedure middleware. On top of that, refuse (not silently ignore) a request that names a different tenant in body or query, and hand handlers a sanitised snapshot of the query object taken once, because the framework may define `req.query` as a getter that re-parses on every access.
  - *Invariant:* Routing the public surface through the internal RPC context makes tenant isolation a rule every handler must remember; routing it through shared services makes it a property of the call graph — the request never reaches the code that would read a tenant id from input. The same sharing is what makes a public write emit the same change events and enqueue the same outbound deliveries as an internal one; anything under the public surface that re-implements a business rule is a bug. Separately: deleting a key off a re-parsing query getter is undone by the next read.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/api/v1/index.ts:1-12, packages/server/src/api/v1/auth.ts:27-40, :162-193, docs/api.md:10-24`
- [ ] **Rate-limit on three keys, fail open, and report the binding budget on every response**
  - Fixed 60s windows counted in the shared store (INCR plus a first-write EXPIRE only), with three counters: per credential; per tenant at a small bounded multiple; and a pre-authentication failure budget keyed on source address AND the presented credential prefix, READ before the credential lookup and charged only on failure. Both authenticated counters are charged on every request. Report the TIGHTER of the two as `RateLimit-Limit/Remaining/Reset`, on successes as well as 429s, with `Retry-After` on the refusal. Provide an in-process fallback for deployments with no shared store: separate maps for authenticated and failure counters, each with a hard entry ceiling that falls open, swept on window rollover rather than on write.
  - *Invariant:* Seven separate quiet failures. (1) Keyed on credential alone, a hundred credentials buy a hundred budgets. (2) Keyed on address alone, one revoked credential's retrying cron refuses every other integration behind the same NAT or egress pool — and anyone who knows a victim shares an address can burn it deliberately. (3) Metering only after successful authentication leaves the cheap half unmetered: a well-formed bogus credential buys an indexed lookup per request for free. (4) Re-issuing the TTL on every write slides the window forever and the key never expires. (5) A store error turned into a 429 is a larger outage than the one being prevented — fall open. (6) A fallback map any unauthenticated caller can grow, swept per write, turns every request into a linear scan that blocks the event loop; sweeping on rollover makes it O(1) amortised. (7) Sharing one fallback map between authenticated and failure counters lets unauthenticated junk fill it to the ceiling and thereby switch the authenticated limiter off. Finally, advertising the looser of two budgets makes a well-behaved client pace itself against a limit it does not have.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/api/v1/rate-limit.ts:1-27, :33-57, :98-131, :190-200, :288-315, :341-368, packages/server/src/api/v1/auth.ts:62-120`
- [ ] **Wrap every success in an envelope, and page with opaque keyset cursors**
  - Single resources answer `{ data }`; pages answer `{ data, nextCursor }` with `nextCursor` ALWAYS present and `null` on the last page. Cursors are base64url over `<sortKey><sep><id>`, decoded by splitting on the FIRST separator, returning null for anything unparseable. Never an offset. Maintain an explicit inventory of which routes are list-shaped and assert envelopes against it.
  - *Invariant:* Omitting `nextCursor` on the last page makes 'you reached the end' and 'the serializer dropped a field' the same wire shape — a paging client stops early and reports a short total, silently. A cursor is the one piece of API state a caller holds across requests, so a decode bug does not error, it pages from the wrong place. Splitting from the right cuts an id that contains the separator in half. The moment a caller can read the sort key out of a cursor, hand-built cursors make it part of the contract. An offset page silently repeats or drops rows whenever anything is written between two requests, which for an append-heavy collection is always.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/api/v1/envelope.ts:1-28, packages/server/src/services/cursor.ts:20-31, :42-69, packages/server/src/api/v1/routes-table.ts:750-768`
- [ ] **Coerce query strings from the validating schema, not by guessing**
  - Everything in a query string is a string, while the shared schemas expect real numbers, booleans and arrays. Read the expected shape per field off the schema's own JSON Schema (the same call the spec generator uses, cached per schema object in a WeakMap), then coerce accordingly; accept both repeated keys and one comma-separated key for arrays; pass an uncoercible value through UNCHANGED so the validator produces the field-naming error. Normalise an absent body to an empty object in one shared helper.
  - *Invariant:* Do not fork a second, 'REST-flavoured' schema set — that is how two surfaces start disagreeing about what a boundary value means. Do not guess by value shape: blind numeric coercion rewrites a numeric-looking search term into a number and strips a leading zero that was part of an id. Do not invent NaN for an uncoercible value — it surfaces later as a mystery 500 deep inside a service instead of a 400 naming the field. And when the framework leaves an absent body undefined, handing that straight to a schema of all-optional fields answers 400 for the most obvious call in the API.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/api/v1/query.ts:1-17, :62-85, :100-150, :151-165`
- [ ] **Split outbound event delivery into enqueue-now and send-later**
  - A mutation calls a fire-and-forget emitter that writes one pending delivery row per interested subscription and returns; a background sweeper on a short interval drains the queue. The emitter mints the row's id itself so the id can travel inside the payload. Store the UNPROJECTED payload on the row. The sweeper is idempotent to start, guarded against overlapping passes, skipped under test, and its timer is unref'd. Each pass claims a bounded batch by pushing the due-time into the future (a lease), and each send additionally compare-and-swaps the attempt counter.
  - *Invariant:* An outbound integration is a side effect of a mutation, never a precondition of it — never await the emitter into a mutation's failure path, or a dead delivery table makes the core write fail. A plain status read does not prevent double-posting: nothing writes a terminal status until the response returns, so two concurrent senders both read 'pending', both pass, and both POST; only an atomic claim on a value that changes (the attempt number, plus the lease) stops them. The attempt counter therefore counts attempts STARTED — a process that dies mid-flight has spent one, and the row becomes due again when the lease expires rather than replaying for free. A background interval in a test process turns a passing suite into a hanging one. Without an in-flight guard, passes stack up while one waits on a slow receiver and the process holds far more concurrent requests than the batch size implies.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/services/webhooks/emit.ts:1-24, packages/server/src/services/webhooks/sweeper.ts:1-60, packages/server/src/services/webhooks/delivery.ts:333-360, :500-540`
- [ ] **Sign outbound payloads over a body serialized exactly once**
  - HMAC-SHA256 with the subscription's secret over `<unix-seconds>.<rawBody>`, hex, sent as `v1=<hex>` in a versioned signature header, beside an event header, a delivery-id header and a timestamp header. Compute the body string ONCE, sign that string, transmit that string. The subscription secret is CSPRNG, shown once at creation, and absent from the wire type by construction (build a fresh object, never spread the document).
  - *Invariant:* Re-serializing the payload between signing and sending is the failure mode: JSON serialization gives no key-ordering guarantee across object identities, so one reordered key makes every receiver that is doing its job reject the delivery as forged — and it fails at the receiver, where you cannot see it. The timestamp must be INSIDE the signed string, not merely a sibling header, or a captured delivery is replayable tomorrow with the header rewritten. Versioning the signature value (`v1=`) is what makes a future algorithm change possible without breaking every receiver at once.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/services/webhooks/signature.ts:1-37, packages/server/src/services/webhooks/delivery.ts:455-482, packages/server/src/trpc/routers/webhooks.ts:5-10, packages/server/src/models/WebhookSubscription.ts:1-6`
- [ ] **Treat every caller-supplied destination URL as an SSRF vector, on every attempt**
  - Validate the URL at create AND at update AND again immediately before each delivery attempt. Keep the resolved addresses and connect to one as a LITERAL using the low-level HTTP client, carrying the original name in the Host header and as the TLS servername (so the certificate is still validated against the name). Refuse non-https and every private/special-use range — loopback, link-local, RFC1918, CGNAT, multicast, reserved and their v6 equivalents — decided on the PARSED BYTES of the address, with v4-in-v6 embeddings unwrapped and run through the v4 table. Reject the host if ANY resolved address is blocked, not just the first. Never follow redirects; record a 3xx as a failure. Disable connection pooling. One env flag lifts the rules for a local listener and belongs nowhere else.
  - *Invariant:* A create-time-only check is decorative against DNS rebinding, and so is check-then-connect: a standard fetch resolves the name a second time, so the address validated is not the address dialled. With a zero-TTL record the attacker answers public for the check and an internal address milliseconds later, and the signed payload is POSTed into the private network — with the response status landing on a delivery row the attacker can read back, i.e. a port scanner. Following a redirect hands back exactly the SSRF the pinning removes: a Location header is a second destination chosen by the receiver and validated by nobody. A pooled keep-alive socket outlives the check that approved it. And a guard that pattern-matches the dotted-quad spelling of a v4-mapped v6 address never fires, because the URL parser re-serializes it to shortest hex form.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/services/webhooks/ssrf.ts:1-10, :30-57, packages/server/src/services/webhooks/delivery.ts:64-80, :82-130, :150-200, :425-438`
- [ ] **Put the retry schedule in a pure table and auto-disable on dead deliveries, not dead attempts**
  - An exported array of delays indexed by attempts-already-made; the maximum attempt count is derived from its length; a `null` return is the single terminal signal. A per-attempt timeout. Increment the subscription's consecutive-failure counter only when a delivery is EXHAUSTED, reset it on the first success, and disable the subscription past a threshold that lives next to the field it governs and is imported, never restated. Record the outcome fields (status, attempt, response status, error) on the delivery row, storing your own error text rather than the receiver's body, and expire the log on a TTL.
  - *Invariant:* A caller that compares the attempt count against a hard-coded number somewhere else is how the table and the give-up point stop agreeing. Counting consecutive failures per ATTEMPT rather than per delivery switches a healthy endpoint off after two and a half bad ones. Two copies of the threshold drift the first time somebody tunes one. Storing the receiver's response body can mean megabytes of HTML on a log row. A subscription nobody owns any more otherwise keeps this server dialling a host that may since have been reassigned to somebody else.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/services/webhooks/backoff.ts:1-61, packages/server/src/models/WebhookSubscription.ts:10-18, packages/server/src/services/webhooks/delivery.ts:265-308, packages/server/src/models/WebhookDelivery.ts:22-24`
- [ ] **Re-ask the authorization question at send time, per subscriber, with an exhaustive switch**
  - A subscription is a STANDING grant, so authorize each delivery against the subscription owner's LIVE permissions, read per delivery, rather than freezing the answer at subscribe time. A pure, exported, unit-tested projection function decides: deliver as-is, deliver with fields stripped, or withhold entirely. Withheld is a first-class terminal outcome distinct from failure — not retried, not counted toward auto-disable. An owner who is no longer a member yields withhold (fail closed, including for rows written before the owner field existed). The switch over payload kinds has NO default arm.
  - *Invariant:* A default arm gives a newly added payload kind a behaviour by accident — 'deliverable' — and ships data the subscriber was never authorized to see; the absence of a default turns that into a compile error. Retrying a withheld delivery can never succeed, and counting it as a failure auto-disables a perfectly healthy endpoint as soon as a colleague acts. Where existence itself is the protected fact, the payload must be withheld entirely rather than stripped — stripping still confirms the thing happened. A payload whose deleted subject is gone must carry the attribute the authorization check needs, or the check cannot run and the delivery can only be dropped.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/services/webhooks/projection.ts:1-30, :45-76, packages/server/src/services/webhooks/delivery.ts:230-252, packages/shared/src/webhooks.ts:52-70`
- [ ] **Scope integration subscriptions to their creator, with no admin override**
  - Every query on the subscription management surface carries `createdBy: <caller>` beside the tenant id; a colleague's subscription answers not-found. Re-validate the destination URL on update as well as create. Ship no tenant-wide list and no admin override. Cleanup on member departure is implicit: the owner's absence makes every delivery withhold.
  - *Invariant:* Because deliveries are projected against the CREATOR's permissions, an editable URL on somebody else's subscription is an exfiltration primitive, not an edit: a lower-privileged member repoints a higher-privileged member's subscription at a host they control and receives payloads projected against the other person, signed by this server. A tenant-wide list is equally unsafe in the other direction — a destination URL is frequently itself a credential (the token sits in the path), so 'oversight' hands over a colleague's secret. Validating the URL only on create lets an endpoint be repointed at a private address one edit later.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/trpc/routers/webhooks.ts:1-35, packages/server/src/trpc/routers/api-tokens.ts:1-28`
- [ ] **Add the integer API-level handshake and enforce its floor ahead of everything else**
  - Both sides declare an integer API level in a request header beside a client-version header; a changelog array records what each level added; the server publishes its level and its minimum-supported level on the health endpoint. The public surface checks the floor in middleware mounted BEFORE every route and before credential authentication, and refuses with 412 and its own error slug. A request that declares NO level is always served. The spec route is exempt from the check. Never attach these headers to the health endpoint.
  - *Invariant:* A release number cannot say whether two peers understand each other — a self-hosted server lags, store-distributed clients lead, a desktop build or an open tab trails, and a server and client of the same release were never guaranteed to deploy together. 412 and not 400: a client that retries queued work treats 400 as 'never send this again', so version skew spelled as 400 deletes user data. The floor refuses only peers that can say how old they are; refusing peers that declare nothing locks out everyone from before the handshake existed. And adding a custom header to the health endpoint forces a CORS preflight that an untrusted origin fails, which reads to the user as 'server unreachable'.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/api/v1/index.ts:123-134, packages/server/src/auth/client-version.ts:1-10, :54-67, packages/server/src/api/v1/problem.ts:224-238, packages/shared/src/api-level.ts:1-26, CLAUDE.md:2315-2345`
- [ ] **Commit a contract snapshot of the internal RPC surface and classify every diff**
  - Emit a byte-stable JSON snapshot holding every procedure's path, kind and input JSON Schema, every push-event kind with its payload schema, and both API levels. Commit it. A test regenerates and compares, and on a difference classifies it as breaking (raise both the level and the floor), additive (bump the level and add a changelog row) or neutral (regenerate only), then fails with that instruction. Compare inputs from the server's side and push-payloads from the client's side. Exclude outputs.
  - *Invariant:* Inputs are what the server actually refuses a request with; outputs are types no client validates, so snapshotting them produces noise. Removing an input property is NEUTRAL, not breaking, because unknown keys are stripped rather than refused — getting that classification wrong forces unnecessary floor raises that strand old clients. A changed pattern or format cannot be ordered, so it must read as breaking. And the snapshot must be byte-stable (recursively sorted keys) or every regeneration is a spurious diff.
  - *Reference:* `packages/server/src/contract/trpc-contract.ts:1-17, :44-46, :57-72, packages/server/src/contract/emit-trpc-contract.ts:1-16, docs/versioning.md:59-90`
- [ ] **Ship integrator-facing docs separate from maintainer docs, including a correct receiver example**
  - Two audiences, two homes: a maintainer page explaining the architectural rules, and a published docs section with overview, authentication, errors, rate limits, the generated reference and the webhook page. The webhook page carries the header table, the payload shape, the full retry schedule, the auto-disable threshold, the log retention, the destination policy, and a working verification snippet that reads the RAW bytes, enforces a timestamp tolerance, and compares constant-time after a length check. State the dedup key and that it is the same id in the header, the payload and the log row.
  - *Invariant:* Three things a naive receiver gets wrong, each of which the example exists to prevent: parsing-then-reserializing the body (every signature fails), skipping the timestamp tolerance (yesterday's captured delivery replays), and comparing hex strings with `===` (leaks the comparison position; and the constant-time primitive throws on a length mismatch, so the length check must come first). Separately: one id space across header, payload and log, or an integrator handed a dedup key cannot look up the failing delivery anywhere in the product.
  - *Reference:* `docs/api.md:1-8, :116-124, docs-site/docs/api/webhooks.md:120-175`

### Identifiers this area freezes

Every name below becomes a contract the first time this area ships. All of
them must come from the manifest's `identifiers` block — see
`cli/src/scaffold/identifiers.ts`. An area that spells one of these out as a
literal has re-introduced the divergence that block exists to prevent.

- API base path and version segment (`/api/v1`) — baked into the spec document, every integrator's base URL and the generated reference page
- Credential scheme prefix (`tt_`) — parsed by fixed length and position, so its character count is part of the parser; already-issued credentials carry it
- Credential prefix/secret byte lengths (6 bytes → 8 chars, 32 bytes → 43 chars) — the total length is a validity check on every presented credential
- Problem `type` URI base (`https://<docs-host>/problems/`) — dereferenced by clients; changing the host orphans every documented failure link
- Problem slugs (`invalid-token`, `insufficient-scope`, `rate-limited`, `not-found`, `client-too-old`, `workspace-not-addressable`, `internal-error`) — integrators branch on them
- Outbound webhook header prefix (`X-<Product>-`) and the four header names beneath it (Event, Delivery, Timestamp, Signature) — already resolved by hatchkit as `identifiers.webhookHeaderPrefix` (cli/src/scaffold/identifiers.ts:107-110)
- Webhook signature version tag (`v1=`) — every deployed receiver strips exactly this prefix
- Client-identity header (`x-<token>-client`) — already resolved as `identifiers.clientHeader` (cli/src/scaffold/identifiers.ts:103-106)
- Version-handshake header names (`x-<token>-client-version`, `x-<token>-api-level`) — sent by every shipped client build
- Per-host device-flow client ids (`identifiers.clientIds`, cli/src/scaffold/identifiers.ts:111-113) — a token already issued to a client carries its id
- Scope string values — written into stored credentials, so renaming one silently un-authorizes every credential holding the old spelling
- Env var names gating this area (`API_RATE_LIMIT_PER_MINUTE`, `WEBHOOK_ALLOW_PRIVATE_TARGETS`, `TRUST_PROXY_HOPS`) — under `identifiers.envPrefix`, present in every self-hoster's .env
- The published API host in the spec's `servers[].url` (e.g. `api.<domain>`) — generated clients bake it in
- Generated operation ids (method + path-derived) — a client generator names its functions after them, so the derivation rule must not change or every call site renames
- Committed artifact paths (`docs-site/static/openapi.json`, `docs-site/docs/api/reference.md`) — the docs site serves them by URL
- Rate-limit response header names (`RateLimit-Limit/Remaining/Reset`, `Retry-After`) and the `application/problem+json` media type
- Redis key prefix for the rate-limit counters (`ratelimit:`) — shared across replicas of a running deployment
- The delivery id format (the queue row's own id), since it is simultaneously the payload id, the header value and the log row key

### Domain-specific — do not extract

- Money projection semantics: nulling a colleague's `hourlyRate`, nulling `progress`, and answering `403 money-visibility-required` on report routes rather than inventing a total (packages/server/src/api/v1/problem.ts:188-214, docs/api.md:61-77). The GENERIC part — a per-caller projection function shared by the read routes and the push stream, and a refusal rather than a silently stripped aggregate — is captured in the gaps; the `canViewOthersMoney` / `canViewOthersTime` flags and the rate/total fields are domain.
- The deliberate absence of invoice, CSV and PDF routes from v1 because they are 'money end to end' (docs/api.md:77-78).
- The concrete event catalogue (`entry.started` / `entry.stopped` / `entry.created` / `entry.updated` / `entry.deleted` / `invoice.created` / `invoice.status_changed`) and the argument that a timer boundary deserves its own event rather than being filtered out of a generic update stream (packages/shared/src/webhooks.ts:18-27).
- The concrete scope names `entries:read`, `entries:write`, `catalog:read`, `catalog:write`, `reports:read` (packages/shared/src/api-tokens.ts:17-23). The mechanism (a flat scope list, read/write never implying each other) is generic; these five strings are not.
- The specific ordering hazard `/entries/current` before `/entries/:id` (packages/server/src/api/v1/index.ts:136-140). De-domained to 'a literal segment must be mounted before the parameterized sibling' in the first gap; the route names themselves are domain.
- `resolveHourlyRate` being write-path only and the rule 'never recompute an amount at read time' (docs/api.md:78).
- The two legacy cursor encodings that predate the shared module — the entry list's own encoder and the report route's encoder shared with the internal layer (packages/server/src/services/cursor.ts:4-19). The generalizable lesson (a shared module that claims to be the single source of truth and is not is worse than one that claims nothing; in-flight cursors are the one piece of state a deploy cannot migrate) is worth keeping, but it only arises once a second encoder exists, which a fresh scaffold does not have.
- The stdio integration server's bare-date resolution rule — that a range end is read as start-of-day by one route and end-of-day by another, so the tool sends full timestamps computed in the caller's zone (CLAUDE.md:2026-2032). Calendar-day semantics are the reference's domain.
- The 24-hex-character shape of the delivery id and the invoice/issuer/recipient payload field inventory in the integrator docs (docs-site/docs/api/webhooks.md:36-110).
- The full API_LEVEL_CHANGES history rows (task colors, avatars, invoice line kinds, business logos) — the changelog MECHANISM is generic, the entries are domain (packages/shared/src/api-level.ts:44-92).

### Reference evidence

- Single route table that BOTH the HTTP router and the spec generator are built from; a route not in it is not mounted, a route in it with no handler throws at boot rather than 404-ing in production — `packages/server/src/api/v1/routes-table.ts:1-14, packages/server/src/api/v1/index.ts:104-121`
- Mount order is load-bearing: literal segments registered before parameterized ones, asserted by a test that also writes out every route's required scope by hand — `packages/server/src/api/v1/index.ts:136-140, packages/server/src/tests/rest-openapi.test.ts:1-30`
- OpenAPI 3.1 document generated from the same zod schemas the handlers validate with (native z.toJSONSchema, no second schema library); stable operationIds derived from method+path; rate-limit headers documented on 200 as well as 429; error responses enumerated per operation — `packages/server/src/api/v1/openapi.ts:1-16, :43-57, :140-186`
- Two generated artifacts (spec JSON + reference page) are COMMITTED and a test fails when either is stale — `packages/server/src/api/v1/emit-openapi.ts:1-13, packages/server/src/tests/openapi-document.test.ts:1-26`
- RFC 9457 problem+json layer owned by the API surface; 5xx detail is a fixed string in every environment because the app's global handler echoes err.message outside production; tRPC-code→status map deliberately incomplete so unmapped codes fall to 500 — `packages/server/src/api/v1/problem.ts:1-8, :30-36, :52-68`
- Existence-hiding status policy: cross-tenant/foreign ids answer 404 never 403; a single 401 body for every credential failure variant so the endpoint is not an oracle — `packages/server/src/api/v1/problem.ts:176-186, packages/server/src/api/v1/auth.ts:44-61, packages/server/src/auth/api-token.ts:130-141`
- Credential format `tt_<8-char prefix>_<43-char secret>`, parsed by position not by splitting on the separator; sha256 over the WHOLE plaintext (not the secret half); constant-time compare with a length assertion first; plaintext returned exactly once and never stored — `packages/server/src/auth/api-token.ts:20-30, :41-56, :70-84, :92-110, packages/server/src/models/ApiToken.ts:1-8`
- Deny-by-default scopes as a property of the check function, never a default value; write does not imply read — `packages/server/src/auth/api-token.ts:113-127, packages/shared/src/api-tokens.ts:11-24`
- Long-lived credential authorization = LIVE permissions intersected with a ceiling frozen at mint time; revocation narrows on the next request, a later grant never widens; a removed member's credential is 401 (dead), not 403 — `packages/server/src/models/ApiToken.ts:56-72, packages/server/src/auth/api-token.ts:175-186`
- A credential can never name a tenant of its own: structural (the request never reaches the tenant resolver) plus a belt-and-braces scrub that refuses a mismatching tenant id in body or query, and a re-read-safe copy of the query object because Express 5's req.query is a re-parsing getter — `packages/server/src/api/v1/auth.ts:27-40, :162-193, packages/server/src/api/v1/index.ts:6-12`
- Three rate-limit counters: per credential, per tenant at a bounded multiple (so minting credentials does not multiply budget), and a PRE-authentication failure budget keyed on address+presented prefix, read before the credential lookup; fail-open on backing-store error; bounded in-memory fallback swept on window rollover; RateLimit-* headers on every authenticated response — `packages/server/src/api/v1/rate-limit.ts:1-27, :33-57, :98-131, :288-315, :341-368, packages/server/src/api/v1/auth.ts:96-120, :125-160`
- Success envelope `{data}` / `{data, nextCursor}` with nextCursor always present and null on the last page — `packages/server/src/api/v1/envelope.ts:1-28`
- Opaque base64url keyset cursors, split on the FIRST separator, decode to null not throw, never an offset; the module's header names the two routes that keep their own encoding rather than claiming to be the only one — `packages/server/src/services/cursor.ts:1-31, :42-69, packages/server/src/tests/api-cursor.test.ts:1-8`
- Schema-directed query-string coercion derived from the same JSON Schema the spec is built from, so a field cannot be coerced one way and documented another; uncoercible values pass through unchanged so zod produces the error — `packages/server/src/api/v1/query.ts:1-17, :100-110, packages/server/src/api/v1/query.ts:151-165`
- Outbound webhook emission is enqueue-only and fire-and-forget on the request path; the unprojected envelope is stored; the row's id is minted by the emitter so one id serves as envelope id, delivery header and log row id — `packages/server/src/services/webhooks/emit.ts:1-24, :44-53`
- Background sweeper with an atomic lease claim, a compare-and-swap on the attempt number so two senders cannot double-post, sequential sends, idempotent start, disabled under test, unref'd timer — `packages/server/src/services/webhooks/sweeper.ts:1-60, packages/server/src/services/webhooks/delivery.ts:333-360, :500-527`
- HMAC-SHA256 over `<timestamp>.<rawBody>` sent as `v1=<hex>`, with the raw body serialized ONCE and handed to the transport unchanged — `packages/server/src/services/webhooks/signature.ts:1-37, packages/server/src/services/webhooks/delivery.ts:455-466`
- SSRF defense: URL validated at create AND before every attempt (DNS rebinding), approved literal addresses pinned and dialled directly via node:http(s) so the socket goes where the check went, Host/SNI keep the original name, redirects never followed (3xx recorded as a failure), agent:false so no pooled socket outlives its check, IPv6 decided on parsed BYTES because WHATWG URL re-serializes mapped addresses, every resolved address checked not just the first — `packages/server/src/services/webhooks/delivery.ts:64-80, :82-130, :150-200, :425-438, packages/server/src/services/webhooks/ssrf.ts:1-10, :30-57`
- Retry schedule as a pure table with the max-attempt count derived from it; auto-disable counted in exhausted DELIVERIES not attempts; threshold imported from the model rather than restated — `packages/server/src/services/webhooks/backoff.ts:1-61, packages/server/src/models/WebhookSubscription.ts:10-18, packages/server/src/services/webhooks/delivery.ts:265-308`
- Per-subscriber authorization projection applied at SEND time against the owner's live permissions, with an exhaustive switch and NO default arm so a new event kind fails to compile rather than defaulting to deliverable; a withheld delivery is a terminal non-failure outcome that is not retried and does not count toward auto-disable — `packages/server/src/services/webhooks/projection.ts:1-30, :45-76, packages/shared/src/webhooks.ts:52-70, packages/server/src/services/webhooks/delivery.ts:230-252`
- Subscription management surface: signing secret returned once and absent from the wire type by construction; URL revalidated on update as well as create; a subscription is managed only by its creator with no admin override and no tenant-wide list, because an editable URL on somebody else's subscription is an exfiltration channel — `packages/server/src/trpc/routers/webhooks.ts:1-35, packages/server/src/models/WebhookSubscription.ts:1-6, :74-90, packages/shared/src/webhooks.ts:30-50`
- Credential management surface: per-member scoping with no admin override, collision re-roll on the unique prefix index, and a hard cap on live credentials per member because the per-credential limiter is only a limiter while the count is bounded — `packages/server/src/trpc/routers/api-tokens.ts:1-28, :44-66`
- Delivery log is a TTL'd debugging record (30 days), stores this server's own error text never the receiver's body, and stores the envelope rather than a reference so it describes what happened when it happened — `packages/server/src/models/WebhookDelivery.ts:1-13, :22-24, packages/server/src/services/webhooks/delivery.ts:485-497`
- Client/server version handshake: an integer API level declared per request, a floor checked AHEAD of every route and ahead of credential authentication, refused as 412 with its own problem slug (never 400, which a retrying offline queue treats as 'never send this again'); a request declaring no level is never refused; the spec route is exempt — `packages/server/src/api/v1/index.ts:123-134, packages/server/src/api/v1/problem.ts:224-238, packages/server/src/auth/client-version.ts:1-10, :54-67, packages/shared/src/api-level.ts:1-26`
- Committed contract snapshot of the internal RPC surface (every procedure's input JSON Schema, every event kind, both levels), regenerated by a test that classifies a difference as breaking / additive / neutral and says which version action each requires; outputs deliberately excluded because no client validates them — `packages/server/src/contract/trpc-contract.ts:1-17, :44-46, docs/versioning.md:59-90`
- Maintainer-facing and integrator-facing docs are separate; the integrator page carries a receiver verification example that does raw-bytes, timestamp-tolerance and constant-time compare, plus the dedup key and the full retry table — `docs/api.md:1-8, docs-site/docs/api/webhooks.md:120-200, docs-site/docs/api/ (overview, authentication, errors, rate-limits, reference, webhooks)`
- A separate stdio integration server built strictly as a client of the public REST surface, never of the internal RPC layer, with capabilities probed once at start and filtered by the credential's scopes — `packages/mcp (described at CLAUDE.md:2006-2040), ARCHITECTURE.md:56`

<a id="deploy-selfhost-ops"></a>

## Deploy / self-host ops

**Key:** `deploy-selfhost-ops` · **Starter today:** partial · **Open rows:** 20

The reference ships a complete second deployment story alongside the vendor's own PaaS deploy: a pull-only, single-origin compose stack (proxy + app server + static web + datastores) with an opt-in build override and a CI override, a checked-in reverse-proxy config, a commented env example, a versioned multi-arch release pipeline whose floating tags only move after an anonymous-pull smoke boot, an ops CLI compiled into the server image, an external PASS/FAIL/SKIP verification script, a boot-time migration runner, automatic and manual rollback gated on schema readability, and ~1900 lines of operator runbook covering install, backup/restore, upgrade and troubleshooting. The starter has the PaaS half only: one production compose file, a build-and-deploy workflow with a genuinely good post-deploy gate (commit poll, baked-origin pairing, CORS preflight, native-origin sign-in probe), a health endpoint that reports its build commit, and a client image that writes a build-stamp file. It has no second compose variant, no proxy config, no env example for an operator, no published versioned images, no migration runner, no ops CLI, no verification script, no smoke job, no rollback-on-failure, and no backup/upgrade/rollback documentation. `cli/src/deploy/rollback.ts` is scaffold-time cleanup of a partial `create`, not deployment rollback.

### Gaps

- [ ] **Ship a self-host compose variant that pulls published images and cannot silently build**
  - A second production compose file, separate from the PaaS one, declaring a TLS-terminating reverse proxy plus the app server, the static web container and the datastores. Every app service carries `image:` and `pull_policy: missing` and NO `build:` key; only the proxy publishes host ports, everything else uses `expose`; all state is in named volumes. A companion `*.build.yml` override adds `build:` and `pull_policy: build` and retags the images to `<name>:local`.
  - *Invariant:* A service that has both `image:` and a `build:` section makes Compose ignore a failed pull and build from source instead, whatever `pull_policy` says — so a mistyped version tag, an unpublished release or a still-private registry package all turn into a multi-gigabyte bundler build that the kernel OOM-kills on the small box the stack is sized for, with nothing on screen naming the cause. And a local build must never be tagged with the registry name: `pull_policy: missing` then finds it forever and the machine keeps running a local build under a release tag it did not come from.
  - *Reference:* `/Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/docker-compose.selfhost.yml and /Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/docker-compose.selfhost.build.yml:20-33`
- [ ] **Publish a second web-client image with no API origin compiled in**
  - The bundler inlines public env values into the browser bundle at image BUILD time. The operator-facing client image must be built with that origin arg empty, so every call site falls back to a relative path and `window.location.origin`, and it must be published under a package name distinct from the vendor's own client image.
  - *Invariant:* An image built against a URL is permanently bound to that host — a runtime env var on the running container cannot change it. Publishing both builds under one package name means two tags of the same package talk to different hosts, and an operator who pulls the wrong one gets a working-looking sign-in page that posts to a domain they do not own. The starter currently forbids this build outright: its client Dockerfile fails when the origin arg is empty and then greps the compiled bundle to prove the value was inlined, so the self-host variant needs a separate Dockerfile, not a flag.
  - *Reference:* `/Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/packages/client/Dockerfile.selfhost:1-20 and /Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/.github/workflows/release.yml:9-38 (starter counter-evidence: starter/packages/client/Dockerfile:58-61)`
- [ ] **Check a single-origin reverse-proxy config into the repo**
  - One site block terminating TLS for one domain, routing the API prefix and the realtime-upgrade path to the app server and everything else to the static web container, with encoding on, hashed assets cached forever and the build-stamp file marked no-cache.
  - *Invariant:* The upgrade route must be matched WITHOUT stripping its prefix: the server compares the upgrade path literally and destroys the socket on anything else, producing a connection that dies with no error the browser can explain. Proxies that order handlers by file position rather than path specificity will let the static catch-all swallow the session endpoint and answer it with the app's HTML at 200, which reads as a bizarre auth failure rather than the routing bug it is. And nothing but the hashed asset directory may be cached, or a deploy leaves stale HTML pointing at chunks that no longer exist.
  - *Reference:* `/Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/Caddyfile:22-82`
- [ ] **Serve at the domain root, and treat the origin spelling as exact**
  - One env var carries the domain and an optional second carries the full URL; the server derives both the origin it authenticates against and the origin it trusts for cross-origin requests and socket upgrades from that single value.
  - *Invariant:* The realtime URL is derived from the browser's origin, so hosting the app under a subpath yields a socket URL that ignores the subpath and never connects. In production the origin comparison is a literal string match: `localhost` and `127.0.0.1`, a present and an absent port, and http versus https are four different deployments as far as the session cookie and the origin check are concerned, and the symptom is a 403 at sign-in rather than anything that points at routing.
  - *Reference:* `/Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/.env.selfhost.example:20-36 and /Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/docs/self-hosting.md:1848-1878`
- [ ] **Ship a commented operator env example split REQUIRED / VERSION / OPTIONAL**
  - Plain text throughout, two required values with the generation command for the signing secret inline, a pinned version variable, and every optional value commented with what turning it on costs and what stays off while it is empty.
  - *Invariant:* The encrypted-env tooling must be provably inert in the published image — the runtime stage copies no env file, so shipping a private-key variable is a no-op that looks like configuration and hides where the real values live. The version must be pinned rather than floating, or an unattended pull moves an operator onto a release whose notes they never read. Omitting an optional variable and setting it empty are not the same thing where a build arg is concerned.
  - *Reference:* `/Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/.env.selfhost.example:1-58`
- [ ] **Versioned multi-arch release pipeline with a promotion gate**
  - Per-architecture legs on native runners pushing by digest only, a merge job joining the digests into one manifest list under the exact version tag and asserting both architectures and both attestation manifests survived, a smoke job that pulls that exact tag anonymously on each architecture and boots the shipped compose file from it, and only then a promote job that moves the minor-line and latest tags.
  - *Invariant:* The smoke job must carry no registry login and no packages permission: a pull that only works with a token proves nothing about what a stranger sees, and a registry that creates new packages private makes the first publish unusable while every dashboard reports success. Floating tags must move last, so a broken or unpullable image never becomes what `latest` points at, while the exact version tag stays published either way. A dispatch with publishing unchecked must build both images on both architectures and discard the result — that is the only way to test the pipeline without publishing.
  - *Reference:* `/Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/.github/workflows/release.yml:45-66, 390-500, 537-570`
- [ ] **An outside-in verification script the operator runs by hand**
  - One `PASS` / `FAIL` / `SKIP` line per check with a `fix:` line under each failure and a non-zero exit when anything fails. Checks: the DNS record and whether it matches this machine's public address, every compose service running and healthy, a trusted certificate, the plain-HTTP port answering with a redirect, the health endpoint's individual fields including the derived origin, the static root returning HTML, the session endpoint answering JSON rather than HTML, the upgrade path answering unauthorized rather than 404 or 502, and an optional API-token check. It skips the container and IP checks when no compose file is present.
  - *Invariant:* The same script must be downloadable and runnable from the operator's own machine, because that is the only way to prove the cloud provider's firewall lets traffic in — a check that only runs on the box cannot see that. It must read only the domain out of the env file and never print any other value from it. A failure line without a fix line is a bug report the operator cannot act on.
  - *Reference:* `/Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/scripts/selfhost-check.sh, documented at docs/self-hosting.md:571-710`
- [ ] **Compile an ops CLI into the server image**
  - Subcommands for a health/config doctor, out-of-band account creation, password reset, a machine-readable listing of top-level tenancy objects, and migration status / dry-run / apply. Passwords are read from stdin so a script can drive the commands with no TTY.
  - *Invariant:* Every check must be a pure function of the values it is handed — never reading process env or opening a connection itself — so each verdict is testable against a made-up environment; one runtime module binds them to the real process. The doctor must open no connection the server does not already open: no telemetry and no version check of its own. Without an out-of-band account-creation and password-reset path, an instance with no mail transport locks its owner out permanently, and password-reset links printed to the container log become the only way back in.
  - *Reference:* `/Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/packages/server/src/cli/doctor.ts:1-13 and /Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/docs/self-hosting.md:902-1035`
- [ ] **Run migrations at boot behind a readability check that precedes the lock**
  - A numbered append-only registry of idempotent migrations executed on the raw driver, each recording the oldest schema version that can still read the data afterwards; a lock document so a second process waits rather than racing; then an index build with a small critical set that stops the boot and everything else warning. The same state is readable through the ops CLI and the doctor.
  - *Invariant:* The readability check must run BEFORE the lock is taken, so a too-old build exits without touching the store and without waiting on a newer build's lock. A migration that fails must not be recorded, so the next start re-runs it — which is also why every migration must be idempotent: a process can die between applying and recording. Never call the ORM's index-sync helper; it drops indexes a newer release added. Raising the minimum-reader floor makes every older build refuse to start, which is the point for a breaking change and an outage for an additive one.
  - *Reference:* `/Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/CLAUDE.md:1662-1687 and docs/self-hosting.md:1376-1426`
- [ ] **Gate deployment rollback on whether the older build can still read the migrated store**
  - Before moving the API back to an earlier commit, compare the migration registry of the new commit against the previous commit's, read out of git — which means the deploy checkout needs full history, not a shallow clone. When the new commit carries a migration the older build cannot read, or either registry cannot be read, roll back only the front end and say so in the error.
  - *Invariant:* Unknown is not safe: a commit missing from the checkout must count as "cannot read", not as "fine". A front end one build behind a server one build ahead is a combination the client/server version handshake already supports; a server that refuses to boot is an outage. Going back past such a migration requires the operator's pre-upgrade dump and is a person's decision, never the job's.
  - *Reference:* `/Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/docs/deploy.md:265-284 and CLAUDE.md:2452-2466`
- [ ] **Roll back automatically on a failed deploy, and still fail the run**
  - Read the current image pins first and keep them as the rollback target, pin the new immutable sha and deploy, poll both artefacts until each reports the new commit, run a gate (health plus datastore connectivity, the front end's baked-in API origin, an authenticated-session endpoint, the cross-origin preflight), and re-pin the previous values when any of it fails.
  - *Invariant:* A rollback is never a green run — the commit on the branch is still broken, and a green run hides that. Only a full-sha pin counts as a rollback target: a mutable branch tag already points at the build that just failed, so "restoring" it redeploys the failure. With no target, or when the rollback itself fails its gate, stop and fail loudly; a second automatic attempt against an unknown state is how an outage grows. Deploy and rollback must share one concurrency group and must never be cancelled in progress, or a run stopped between pinning and restoring leaves production on a build nobody verified. The starter pins and gates thoroughly but never restores.
  - *Reference:* `/Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/docs/deploy.md:216-264 (starter counter-evidence: starter/.github/workflows/build-and-deploy.yml:164-240 and 300-386)`
- [ ] **A manual rollback workflow that is the same script with a target argument**
  - Dispatch with a commit and which half of the deployment to move. It resolves an abbreviated sha, refuses one not reachable from the deploy branch, reads the migration registries before touching anything, and on a failed gate leaves the requested target pinned rather than restoring what was there.
  - *Invariant:* Because the manual job shares one concurrency group with the automatic deploy and the queue keeps only one pending run per group, a rollback queued behind a running deploy is silently dropped when a newer push takes the waiting slot — the dispatcher must watch the run to non-zero exit, and nothing re-dispatches it. Restoring the previous pins on failure is the wrong default for a manual rollback: those pins are usually the build being escaped.
  - *Reference:* `/Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/.github/workflows/hosted-rollback.yml and docs/deploy.md:285-335`
- [ ] **Recover open clients whose code-split chunks a deploy deleted**
  - A build-stamp file served no-cache beside the app; after mount, on focus or visibility change and at most every few minutes, the page compares it with the commit compiled into its own bundle and offers a reload. Separately, a failed chunk or dynamic import triggers one reload per minute, guarded in per-tab storage, after which the error screen's button is the only way out.
  - *Invariant:* The reload is never automatic and must wait until no write is in flight, because the offline queue does not persist a write that fails during page unload. Keep the once-per-interval guard on the chunk reload: without it a broken server turns every open tab into a reload loop. Both behaviours must be disabled in the dev server and in every packaged shell, where there is no deploy to notice and the stamp file does not exist.
  - *Reference:* `/Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/CLAUDE.md:2469-2485`
- [ ] **Document backup, restore, and what the backup does not contain**
  - Name the volumes (the compose project name prefixes every one of them, so state it), give one dump command that runs inside the already-running datastore container using tools its image ships, a restore that stops only the app container while leaving the datastore up, and a container-and-tar recipe for the certificate volume with the stack stopped.
  - *Invariant:* One datastore holds both application data AND accounts and sessions, so a single dump is the whole instance — say so, or operators back up half of it. The config file and the session-signing secret are NOT in the dump and cannot be regenerated: losing the secret signs every device out. A drop-and-restore replaces only the collections the archive contains, so anything created after the dump survives — usually, but not always, what the operator wanted. And a backup that lives only on the same machine is not a backup.
  - *Reference:* `/Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/docs/self-hosting.md:1195-1278`
- [ ] **Document the upgrade sequence: move the clone, pull, then restart**
  - The compose file and the proxy config change between releases too, so the operator checks the working copy out at the target tag before changing the pinned version; the pull is a separate step that downloads while the old containers keep serving; only then the restart. Include a table of which tag shapes exist and which one is the vendor's own mutable deploy tag.
  - *Invariant:* A mistyped version tag must cost zero downtime, which only holds when the pull runs as its own step ahead of the restart — it stops with the registry's error before anything is recreated. Take a dump before every upgrade: nothing undoes a migration. The vendor's branch tag must be named in the docs as the thing never to pin, because it is the one tag that looks like a reasonable choice and is not a release.
  - *Reference:* `/Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/docs/self-hosting.md:1279-1375`
- [ ] **Boot the shipped self-host stack in CI on every change to it**
  - A workflow triggered by paths covering the self-host compose file, its CI override, the proxy config, both Dockerfiles and the ops CLI. It builds both images from the checkout and loads them under local tags, validates the merged compose file, boots with `up -d --wait`, hits the health endpoint THROUGH the proxy over TLS, runs the doctor inside the container, creates an account with the ops CLI, signs in through the proxy, resets the password and asserts the old session died. Nothing is pushed and no secret is needed — the signing secret is generated and masked per run.
  - *Invariant:* The CI override may change only image tags and healthcheck intervals; the probes must stay the base file's own, or a green run proves the boot of something other than what operators run. Values the base file demands with a required-variable interpolation cannot come from an override — Compose interpolates before merging files — so the workflow must export them. Without this job the self-host stack is read by nothing else in CI (the e2e suite runs the processes directly and the vendor deploy uses different compose files), so a break is found by the first person who tries to install it.
  - *Reference:* `/Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/.github/workflows/selfhost-smoke.yml and docker-compose.selfhost.ci.yml`
- [ ] **Healthcheck every service, and make the proxy wait on them**
  - A healthcheck on each service, `start_interval` set alongside `start_period` so the first probe runs seconds after start rather than one full interval later, and the proxy declaring `service_healthy` on both app containers.
  - *Invariant:* The probe must run on loopback inside the container and must use a method the endpoint actually accepts — a HEAD-style spider against an admin endpoint that answers 405 outside its method list marks a proxy that is routing traffic perfectly as unhealthy forever. It cannot go through the public port either: a site block bound to the configured domain answers a probe carrying any other Host header with 404 by design. Without the short start interval, a service ready in three seconds still reports `starting` for thirty and everything gated on it waits with it. Compose refuses the start interval without the start period, and older engines ignore the field rather than failing, so the stack still starts either way.
  - *Reference:* `/Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/docker-compose.selfhost.yml (caddy and server healthcheck blocks)`
- [ ] **Make a self-hosted instance silent by default**
  - Analytics and third-party SDK build args are omitted from the self-host image build rather than passed empty, so no third-party script tag is emitted at all. Any check for a newer release is opt-in behind one env var, runs at most daily, reads a public endpoint and updates nothing.
  - *Invariant:* Omitting a build variable and passing it empty are different outcomes — an empty value can still ship a disabled SDK that phones home on load. Default off, state it where the operator decides, and make the doctor read the stored result of the opt-in check rather than performing a check of its own.
  - *Reference:* `/Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/.github/workflows/release.yml:33-37 and docker-compose.selfhost.yml (the update-check variable in the server environment block)`
- [ ] **State the single-replica invariant where it is read**
  - When realtime fan-out is in-process with no cross-process bridge, the compose file runs exactly one replica of the app server, and both the file and the operator docs say why.
  - *Invariant:* A second replica does not error. It delivers live updates only to the devices that happened to connect to the same process, which presents forever as an intermittent sync bug rather than as a scaling mistake.
  - *Reference:* `/Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/docker-compose.selfhost.yml (server service header comment) and docs/self-hosting.md:118-123`
- [ ] **Write the operator runbook the stack is useless without**
  - A numbered install doc with a Run/Expect pair per step covering host prep, firewall, DNS, swap, the env file, the first start and verification; an 'install with an assistant' prompt plus a table of what the assistant must already have; a sizing table; a local-trial section; symptom-keyed troubleshooting with the fix for each failure the verification script can emit; and a closing table naming every file involved and its role.
  - *Invariant:* Build mode needs several times the RAM of run mode, so the two figures must be given separately or operators size the machine for the wrong one and the build is OOM-killed with no explanation. Secrets stay on the server: the secret-generation step writes straight into the env file without printing it, and the doc says not to paste that file into a conversation. Every failure the verification script can print needs a matching section, or the fix line is the end of the trail.
  - *Reference:* `/Users/rico/projects/trackyourtime/docs/self-hosting.md:54-145 and 1693-1938`

### Identifiers this area freezes

Every name below becomes a contract the first time this area ships. All of
them must come from the manifest's `identifiers` block — see
`cli/src/scaffold/identifiers.ts`. An area that spells one of these out as a
literal has re-introduced the divergence that block exists to prevent.

- Registry package names for each published image, including the distinct suffix that separates the vendor-origin client build from the no-origin self-host client build — a rename orphans every operator's pinned tag and every pull command in the docs
- The compose project `name:` — it prefixes every named volume, so the backup/restore docs, the `docker volume ls` filter and every tar recipe quote it literally
- Service names inside each compose file — the PaaS keys its per-service domain mapping by them, and a mismatch yields a 503 for the site while the PaaS API reports 200
- The version-pin env var (`<ENVPREFIX>_VERSION`) — it is in every self-hoster's env file, so a rename breaks their next upgrade
- The domain and full-URL env var names (`APP_DOMAIN`, `APP_URL`) and the session-signing secret env var name — the secret's value cannot be regenerated, and changing the name strands it
- The image-pin env var names the deploy job writes onto the PaaS app (`SERVER_IMAGE` / `CLIENT_IMAGE` / `APP_IMAGE`) — the PaaS env API only updates an existing key, so a pin naming a key that was never created returns 200 and does nothing
- The health endpoint path and its field names, and the build-stamp file path and its field names — the deploy gate, the verification script, the ops doctor and the open-tab reload check all parse them
- The literal realtime upgrade path(s) the server compares against — the proxy rule must match them without stripping, so the path is a contract between the proxy config and the server
- The floating tag names (`latest`, the minor line) and the reserved mutable branch tag — operators pin to these strings and the promote job creates them
- The application database name inside the container connection string — it is in every dump archive and every restore command
- The static-export cache-control paths (the hashed asset directory, the build-stamp file) baked into the client image's own proxy config

### Domain-specific — do not extract

- The product's feature list in the 'what you get' table (time tracking, catalog, tags, reports, invoices, CSV/JSON import-export) — the table shape is generic, its contents are not
- The concrete RAM/disk/CPU figures — they are measured against this app's workload; the template should carry the pull-mode-versus-build-mode SPLIT, not the numbers
- The critical-index list (one running timer per person, one membership per person and workspace, unique invoice numbers, unique scheduled jobs, unique API token prefixes) — the mechanism 'a small set of integrity-guarding indexes stops the boot, everything else warns' is generic; the members are not
- The two one-off scripts (`backfill-email-verified`, `migrate:workspaces`) and the workspace-scoping migration they exist for
- The e-invoice font assets the server runtime stage copies because PDF/A forbids the standard-14 fonts — a real Dockerfile gotcha, but tied to this product's document format
- The docs-site-inside-the-client-image decision (folder of the apex rather than a docs subdomain, the noindex/canonical guard, the sitemap lastmod problem) — real ops knowledge, but it belongs to a docs-publishing area rather than self-host ops
- The specific store-client origins trusted by default (the mobile shells' scheme, the packaged-desktop origin, the pinned extension id, the per-install extension origin matched by shape) and the flags that toggle them — the MECHANISM (a self-host server must trust the product's own packaged clients by default or every sign-in from them is refused before the password is checked) is generic and belongs to the auth/origins area; the origin values are not
- The two-host domain history (why a wildcard certificate covering one label forced a fresh apex zone, and why the path split failed under a Caddy-labelling proxy) — the transferable rule is 'a wildcard covers one label' and 'same-host proxy site blocks merge and a prefix-stripping handler breaks a mounted API', but the narrative is this deployment's
- The newsletter/mailing-list smoke commands listed under the same ops umbrella in the reference's CLAUDE.md

### Reference evidence

- Pull-only self-host compose stack: five services, proxy is the only one publishing ports, every app service has image+pull_policy and deliberately NO build: section, with the reason written out in the file — `docker-compose.selfhost.yml (whole file, 296 lines; server service comment block and pull_policy at the top of that service)`
- Opt-in build override that retags the images to <name>:local and switches pull_policy to build, with the explanation of why a local build must never carry the registry name — `docker-compose.selfhost.build.yml (whole file, 61 lines)`
- CI override changing only image tags and healthcheck intervals, with a note that ${VAR:?} values cannot come from an override because Compose interpolates before merging — `docker-compose.selfhost.ci.yml (whole file, 51 lines)`
- Single-domain reverse-proxy config: API prefix and the realtime upgrade path to the app server, everything else to the static web container; documents that the upgrade rule must not strip its prefix and that handler ordering is by path specificity — `Caddyfile:22-82`
- Web-client image built with an EMPTY public API-origin arg, published under a distinct package name, serving the static export from a small proxy with immutable/no-cache header rules — `packages/client/Dockerfile.selfhost (whole file)`
- Operator env example split into REQUIRED / VERSION / OPTIONAL, all plain text, with the reason the encrypted-env tooling is inert in the published image — `.env.selfhost.example:1-58`
- Release pipeline: per-arch native runners pushing by digest, a merge job creating the manifest list under the exact version tag, an anonymous-pull smoke job on both arches, and a promote job that moves the minor and latest tags only after smoke passes — `.github/workflows/release.yml:45-66, 390-500, 537-570`
- Anonymous-pull release verification script: registry checks with curl only, a throwaway docker config with no auths, a compose boot of the tagged file on a random loopback port, repo-digest proof that images were pulled not built, and optional floating-tag digest comparison — `scripts/verify-release-images.sh:1-60`
- Operator verification script with one PASS/FAIL/SKIP line per check and a fix: line under each failure; degrades to outside-in checks when run off the server — `scripts/selfhost-check.sh (569 lines); documented at docs/self-hosting.md:571-710`
- CI smoke workflow that builds both images from the checkout, boots the shipped compose file with --wait, hits health THROUGH the proxy over TLS, runs doctor in the container, creates an account with the ops CLI, signs in through the proxy, resets the password and asserts the old session died — `.github/workflows/selfhost-smoke.yml (whole file, 170 lines)`
- Ops CLI compiled into the server image: doctor, create-user, reset-password, list-workspaces --json, migrate --status/--dry-run/apply; every check is a pure function of what it is handed and opens no connection the server does not already open — `packages/server/src/cli/doctor.ts:1-13 (and accounts.ts, migrate.ts, runtime.ts in the same directory)`
- Boot-time migration runner: readable-check before the lock, append-only idempotent migrations on the raw driver, a lock document, then index build with a critical set that stops the boot; never calls the ORM index-sync helper — `CLAUDE.md:1662-1687; operator-facing at docs/self-hosting.md:1376-1426`
- Automatic rollback on a failed hosted deploy: read the current sha pins first, pin and deploy, poll both artefacts for the commit, run a four-part gate, re-pin the previous values on failure, and fail the run either way — `docs/deploy.md:216-264; logic in scripts/lib/coolify-deploy.mjs, entry at scripts/coolify-deploy.mjs`
- The server is not rolled back past a migration the previous build cannot read: the two commits' migration registries are compared with git show, and an unreadable registry counts as unsafe — `docs/deploy.md:265-284; CLAUDE.md:2452-2466`
- Manual rollback workflow sharing one concurrency group with the deploy, refusing a sha not reachable from the deploy branch, with restore_on_failure off by default — `.github/workflows/hosted-rollback.yml; docs/deploy.md:285-335`
- Open-tab deploy recovery: a no-cache build-stamp file polled on focus/visibility, a manual reload that waits for writes in flight, and a once-per-60s guarded reload on a failed chunk import — `CLAUDE.md:2469-2485`
- Backup and restore: volume table keyed by the compose project name, a dump command using the tools the datastore image already ships, a restore that stops only the app, a tar recipe for the certificate volume, and an explicit list of what is NOT in the backup — `docs/self-hosting.md:1195-1278`
- Upgrade and rollback runbook: move the clone to the tag first, pull as a separate step, then restart; a table of which tag shapes exist including the vendor's mutable deploy tag that must never be pinned — `docs/self-hosting.md:1279-1375`
- Install runbook with Run/Expect per step, an 'install with an AI assistant' prompt plus what the assistant needs and a keep-secrets-out-of-the-chat warning, a sizing table separating pull-mode from build-mode RAM, a local-trial section, symptom-keyed troubleshooting, and a table of every file involved — `docs/self-hosting.md:54-145, 1693-1938`
- Single-replica invariant stated in the compose file and in the docs, because realtime fan-out is in-process with no cross-process bridge — `docker-compose.selfhost.yml (server service header comment); docs/self-hosting.md:118-123`
